| Quasar features are added by annotating external calls with the relevant effects and changing how internal code is executed to track them. | 2506.12202 — Quasar: A Programming Language Specialized for LLM Code Actions | 1 | New features can then easily be implemented by (1) annotating external calls with the effects relevant to that feature, and (2) modifying the execution of the internal code to track this information. |
| Quasar's three demonstrated features are access control with batched approvals, autoparallelization of external calls, and conformal prediction for uncertainty quantification. | 2506.12202 — Quasar: A Programming Language Specialized for LLM Code Actions | 1 | access control with batched user queries to improve security, autoparallelization of external calls to reduce latency, and conformal prediction for uncertainty quantification to mitigate hallucinations. |

| Full Python is the parent language and the Python subset is the child language. 84.8% (28/33) is the best of two configurations (GPT-3.5-turbo); GPT-4-turbo reached 24/33 (72.7%) and the paper's pooled figure is 78%. The number belongs to the whole pipeline (child-language prompt, MAX-SMT type repair, up to five LLM hole-filling calls), on 33 textbook exercises, while the 0/660 figure comes from the elicitation study on a different set (33 regression tests); the parent-language choice was never ablated on its own, and 'parse' means passing all compiler checks, including type checks. | 2406.03636 — Synthetic Programming Elicitation for Text-to-Code in Very Low-Resource Programming and Formal Languages | 9 | Eudoxus outperforms all baselines in terms of compiler checks (see "Parse Rate" in Table 1), passing all compiler checks on 78% of benchmarks. |
| The agent's tools are documentation navigation, semantic search and a Cangjie type-lookup index, plus an Execute tool that runs code and a Submit tool that runs public tests; it is not a compiler alone. Most of the jump from zero-shot comes from the documentation: single-time RAG already reaches 40.00 / 46.45 / 59.35 and iterative RAG 47.10 / 52.90 / 65.81, so the agentic loop adds 16-21 points over docs-only retrieval. Sampling at temperature 1.0 with no repeated runs or variance reported, on 155 self-contained HumanEval-derived problems. | 2602.06976 — Bridging the Knowledge Void: Inference-time Acquisition of Unfamiliar Programming Languages for Coding Tasks | 5 | \| Qwen3-Max         \| Single-time RAG                         \| 46.45                   \| |
| The ablation (Qwen3-Max, code generation) does not rank the compiler above the documentation: removing the verification tools drops accuracy 73.55 to 53.55, exactly as much as removing semantic search (53.55), and removing TypeLookup drops it to 57.42. Documentation alone took Qwen3-Max from 7.74 to 46.45. The paper's conclusion is that exploration and verification work only together, so the evidence supports 'docs and compiler are complementary', not 'the compiler is the most important half'. | 2602.06976 — Bridging the Knowledge Void: Inference-time Acquisition of Unfamiliar Programming Languages for Coding Tasks | 6 | Without the ability to receive feedback from the execute environment, the accuracy falls dramatically to 53.55%, which is only marginally better than the iterative RAG baseline. |

The Llama 3 statement needs its own citation (the Llama 3 report), not this paper. | 2308.09895 — Knowledge Transfer from High-Resource to Low-Resource Programming Languages for Code LLMs | 18 | The datasets presented in this article are part of the StarCoder2 pretraining corpus as one of several sources of "(Leandro's) High Quality" data. |
| MultiPL-T was measured only on languages already in the model's pretraining data, with the low-resource model itself as translator (50-100 samples per function). The authors say it is unlikely to work as-is for languages the model was never trained on, which is lotml's case. For lotml the translator would have to be a rule-based transpiler or a prompted frontier model, neither of which MultiPL-T tested. | 2308.09895 — Knowledge Transfer from High-Resource to Low-Resource Programming Languages for Code LLMs | 25 | MultiPL-T targets low-resource languages, but it is unlikely to work as-is for "no-resource" languages that StarCoderBase is not trained on at all. |
| Agnostics reports the cost of that path: MultiPL-T's rejection sampling yields a working program for only about 30% of prompts even with 50-100 attempts, and each target needs a hand-written compiler for test cases and signatures that supports only built-in Python types (no classes) mapped faithfully into the target. Those limits apply to a Python-to-lotml pipeline too, and the wiki should state them. | 2508.04865 — Agnostics: Learning to Code in Any Programming Language via Reinforcement with a Universal Learning Environment | 3 | In MultiPL-T, ≈30% of prompts produce a working program for n ∈ {50, 100} attempts, and the rest are discarded. |
| The numbers are Ag-LiveCodeBench-X pass@1 (the wiki does not name the benchmark; on MultiPL-E the same 4B models moved only 61->64 Lua and 51->54 Julia). Training also needed a 5,369-problem I/O dataset, a prompt prefix of language tips (written with OpenAI o3 for OCaml and Fortran), and a nonzero base success rate (0.09% was enough). The method failed to improve Qwen 3 1.7B and Llama 3.2 3B. For a language the model has never seen, a compiler and tests are necessary but not shown to be sufficient. | 2508.04865 — Agnostics: Learning to Code in Any Programming Language via Reinforcement with a Universal Learning Environment | 8 | However, we found that Agnostics training on Ag-Codeforces-X does not improve two smaller models, Qwen 3 1.7B and Llama 3.2 3B Instruct, perhaps due to the problems being too difficult for models of this size. |
| Quasar measures tooling benefits from effect annotations on external calls. Batched approvals cut user interactions by 26% on both GQA and AgentDojo, and autoparallelization cut running time by 18% (GQA) and 27% (AgentDojo). Variance is large because only about half of the tasks can be batched or parallelized. On BrowseComp-Plus the cuts were 97% fewer approvals and a 93% speedup. The caveat is that tool authors attach the annotations to external functions, and the LLM writes plain Python, so this is evidence for tooling, not for models writing effect annotations. | 2506.12202 — Quasar: A Programming Language Specialized for LLM Code Actions | 9 | On the GQA dataset, QUASAR reduces running time by 18%±23. |
| The table leaves out the decisive point for lotml: models do not write Quasar. They write a restricted Python subset (no classes or inheritance) that a transpiler compiles to Quasar, because models struggle to generate Quasar directly. Restricting generation to the subset kept accuracy close to unrestricted Python (GQA 71.4 vs 71.8). The third feature, conformal prediction for uncertainty quantification, is also missing from the table. | 2506.12202 — Quasar: A Programming Language Specialized for LLM Code Actions | 5 | Instead, our strategy is to have the LLM generate Python and then transpile this Python code to QUASAR using an existing compiler (Mell et al., 2026a). |
| Quasar does report measurements: task accuracy when generating through the Python subset against unrestricted Python, reductions in approvals and latency, and conformal coverage. None of them shows that a new surface syntax helps the model, because the model writes Python. 'None with evidence that the design helps the model write code' is accurate; 'none with measured evidence' is not. | 2506.12202 — Quasar: A Programming Language Specialized for LLM Code Actions | 5 | We show that generating QUASAR code via transpilation retains task performance comparable to that of Python, and we present several strategies for improving transpilation success. |

| A conventional type checker cannot drive constrained decoding: the decoder needs a completion engine that answers whether a prefix can still be completed into a well-typed program. | 2504.09246 — Type-Constrained Code Generation with Language Models | 4 | Note that commonly used grammar and type checkers can not be used as a completion engine for constrained decoding. |
| The authors suggest that new languages build their compilers as incremental completion engines, so constrained generation comes for free alongside parsing and type checking. | 2504.09246 — Type-Constrained Code Generation with Language Models | 21 | their compilers as an incremental completion engine, which additionally enables automatic adoption for constrained code generation, besides conventional grammar parsing and type checking. |
| To make the constrainer work, the authors made TypeScript stricter. Parameters and return types must be annotated, every variable must be annotated or initialized, and callbacks to reduce need an annotated accumulator. Without these, generated code often failed to compile. | 2504.09246 — Type-Constrained Code Generation with Language Models | 15 | We have identified three situations where generated code often fails to compile without type annotations, prompting us to enforce them. |
| The TypeScript subset excluded features that block left-to-right typing, among them destructuring assignment, unannotated uninitialized declarations, return-type inference, literal types and enums. | Self-referential members such as valueOf or clone produce unbounded chains of higher-order types. | LLMs writing a low-resource ML-family language borrowed syntax and library functions from the nearby high-resource languages (OCaml, Elm). A Measurement Methodology and Preliminary Study | 10 | pyright resolves references lazily per file, so a cold find references issued at a definition - the natural query for a rename - returns only the definition (1 result) until the referencing files are opened |

| Outlines is an open-source Python library that guides generation with regular expressions and context-free grammars by precomputing an index from finite-state-machine states to vocabulary tokens. | 2307.09702 — Efficient Guided Generation for Large Language Models | 1 | This framework leads to an efficient approach to guiding text generation with regular expressions and context-free grammars by allowing the construction of an index over a language model's vocabulary. |
| The paper's method is implemented in the open-source Python library Outlines and claims little overhead per generated token. | 2307.09702 — Efficient Guided Generation for Large Language Models | 1 | It adds little overhead to the token sequence generation process and significantly outperforms existing solutions. An implementation is provided in the open source Python library Outlines [Louf and Willard]. |

| SynCode reduced the number of syntax errors in generated Python and Go code by 96.07% on average relative to unconstrained generation (CodeGen-350M, WizardCoder-1B and LLaMA-7B on HumanEval and MBXP). | 2403.01632 — SynCode: LLM Generation with Grammar Augmentation | 23 | Furthermore, SynCode reduces the number of syntax errors in generated Python and Go code by 96.07% on average compared to standard generation. |

| SynCode handles Python's indentation outside the context-free grammar, with a mechanism that tracks the acceptable indentation for the next token and masks tokens that violate it. | 2403.01632 — SynCode: LLM Generation with Grammar Augmentation | 16 | To support languages with indentation, such as Python and YAML, SynCode has a mechanism that tracks acceptable indentation for the next token, effectively masking tokens that violate indentation constraints at a given point. |

| Grammar-constrained decoding, and constrained decoding in general, can distort the LLM's distribution: outputs are grammatical but their likelihoods are not proportional to the model's. | 2405.21047 — Grammar-Aligned Decoding | 1 | In this paper we demonstrate that GCD techniques (and in general constrained decoding techniques) can distort the LLM's distribution, leading to outputs that are grammatical but appear with likelihoods that are not proportional to the ones given by the LLM, and so ultimately are low-quality. |
| The paper names the problem grammar-aligned decoding (GAD) and proposes ASAp, which converges to the grammar-conditioned LLM distribution as more samples are drawn. | 2405.21047 — Grammar-Aligned Decoding | 2 | By updating this bound when more samples are observed, the decoding algorithm converges to the desired probability distribution-i.e., it samples outputs from the LLM-induced probability conditioned on the outputs being accepted by the grammar. |
| Grammar-Aligned Decoding was peer-reviewed at NeurIPS. | 2405.21047 — Grammar-Aligned Decoding | 11 | The authors would like to thank NeurIPS anonymous reviewers for their insightful feedback and helpful discussions. |
| Monitor-Guided Decoding appeared at NeurIPS 2023. | 2306.10763 — Guiding Language Models of Code with Global Context using Monitors | 1 | To appear in 37th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2023) as |
| MGD runs a monitor that queries static analysis (a language server over LSP) at trigger points during decoding and turns its suggestions into token masks. | 2306.10763 — Guiding Language Models of Code with Global Context using Monitors | 2 | A monitor observes the code generated by an LM and queries static analysis at pre-defined trigger points. The suggestions returned by the static analysis are converted to masks which are used for reshaping the logits |

Nothing in the paper measures an indentation-sensitive or full programming-language grammar end to end. | 2411.15100 — XGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine for Large Language Models | 9 | The Python DSL covers basic control flow (if, for, while) and data types (str, int, float, bool) but ignores indentation. |

The leftover errors are truncated programs, not violations of the grammar. | 2403.01632 — SynCode: LLM Generation with Grammar Augmentation | 17 | The grammar has commonly used features of the language such as control flow, and loops, and excludes some features such as Python's support for lambda functions. |
| SynCode treats indentation as a non-context-free fragment handled by a decoding-time indentation tracker that masks tokens, not as lexer machinery. It reports no cost for it. The paper's evidence against indentation is residual indentation errors (Table 7), not cost. | 2403.01632 — SynCode: LLM Generation with Grammar Augmentation | 16 | SynCode can handle non-context-free fragments of PLs, such as indentation in Python and end-of-scope markers in Go. |
| GAD is the name of the problem. The algorithm, ASAp, removes the bias only asymptotically, over repeated samples of the same prompt (2,000 per benchmark). | For parser-guided generation, REDUCE operations make the index depend on parser stack configurations, so it needs a trie over stack values. | The runtime cost sits in context-dependent tokens, those that finish a rule and need the full stack to validate. They were under 1% of the vocabulary for JSON, which is why the mask cache works. | Grammar ambiguity (non-determinism, epsilon edges) multiplies parallel stacks, and each stack must check its context-dependent tokens and merge masks. | The comparison is wider than one token per block. With explicit delimiters, layout that leaves the AST unchanged can be stripped from code given to the model as context: 14.7% of tokens in Java files and 13.2% in C# and C++, against 4.0% in Python, whose indentation and newlines are syntax; in fill-in-the-middle tests the stripped input cost no significant accuracy. The saving is input-side only (models keep formatting their output unless prompted or fine-tuned), and the paper measured completion, not editing or lotml. So braces do buy a modest input-token option that significant indentation forecloses. | 2508.13666 — The Hidden Cost of Readability: How Code Formatting Silently Consumes Your LLM Budget | 2 | With the same measurement, we observe a surprisingly non-marginal token reduction for all three languages, with 14.7% for Java, 13.2% for C#, and 13.2% for C++. | Novel symbols also cost accuracy, and a single-token control ruled out tokenization as the cause; new syntax is cheaper than conflicting syntax but not free. | 2510.03415 — LLMs Lean on Priors, Not Programming Language Semantics | 31 | Even when all obfuscated symbols are atomic tokens, models still fail to reliably apply the correct operational rules and compose them over execution. |

| At inference SLMFix calls the small model only when the static validator reports errors, giving it the program, the original prompt and the validator's error messages. | 2511.19422 — SLMFix: Leveraging Small Language Models for Domain Specific Language Error Fixing with Reinforcement Learning | 3 | If there are no errors the program is output and otherwise the program, the original prompt, and the error messages from the validator are provided to the SLM which generates a revised program as the final output. |
| SLMFix's GRPO reward is a weighted average of a boolean static-validator signal and a 0-to-1 semantic (AST-similarity) score against the ground-truth program. | 2511.19422 — SLMFix: Leveraging Small Language Models for Domain Specific Language Error Fixing with Reinforcement Learning | 3 | The static validator generates a boolean signal indicating if the given program contains syntactic errors, while the semantic metric will output a score between 0 and 1 representing how well the program aligns with the input query. The reward of the program is then a weighted average between these two signals |
| SLMFix's training data starts from 20 hand-written query/ground-truth pairs per language, each sampled 10 times from five strong code LLMs to produce erroneous programs to repair. | 2511.19422 — SLMFix: Leveraging Small Language Models for Domain Specific Language Error Fixing with Reinforcement Learning | 6 | From each language-specific dataset, we manually constructed a subset of 20 diverse natural language queries along with corresponding ground truth code implementations as the example set. | FLAME is a T5-small-based model with 60 million parameters, trained only on Excel formulas and compared against models of up to 175B parameters on repair and completion. | 2301.13779 — FLAME: A small language model for spreadsheet formulas | 1 | FLAME is based on T5-small (Raffel et al. 2020) and has only 60 million parameters, yet it can compete with much larger models (up to 175B parameters) on last-mile formula repair and formula completion. |
| FLAME's pre-training corpus was cut from 927M formulas to 6.1M by syntax-based (sketch) deduplication of formulas that differ only in cell inputs. | 2301.13779 — FLAME: A small language model for spreadsheet formulas | 2 | To create a small but varied pre-training corpus, we reduced a corpus of 927M formulas down to 6.1M by comparing formulas based on syntax, removing near-duplicate formulas, which only vary in terms of cell inputs |
| Deduplicating globally instead of per workbook left 591K formulas and gave a worse model, e.g. -8 points on fine-tuned repair and -19 points on exact-match completion. | 2301.13779 — FLAME: A small language model for spreadsheet formulas | 6 | This results in a pre-training set of 591K formulas. Table 4 shows that training on this smaller corpus results in a worse model (e.g., -8pp on fine-tuned repair, -19pp on exact match completion). |
| Repair fine-tuning used no human-labelled repairs: 200K well-formed help-forum formulas were corrupted with FLAME's user-inspired noise operators to make broken/fixed pairs. | 2301.13779 — FLAME: A small language model for spreadsheet formulas | 4 | We create a fine-tuning dataset for all systems by taking 200K well-formed formulas from Excel help forums 3 and randomly applying our user-inspired noise operators to generate broken formulas. |

| Lean Copilot runs model inference inside Lean by wrapping it in a C++ function called through Lean's FFI, either locally via CTranslate2 or on a server. | 2404.12534 — Lean Copilot: Large Language Models as Copilots for Theorem Proving in Lean | 5 | Lean Copilot makes the model runnable in Lean by wrapping its inference into a C++ function operating on strings, which can be called in Lean through FFI. As in Fig. 1, we can run the model either locally via CTranslate2 or on a server. |
| Lean Copilot's default model is LeanDojo's pretrained ReProver, a ByT5 encoder-decoder chosen because it does both tactic generation and premise retrieval; no parameter count is given in this paper. | 2404.12534 — Lean Copilot: Large Language Models as Copilots for Theorem Proving in Lean | 5 | By default, we adopt the pretrained ReProver model from LeanDojo (Yang et al., 2023) for its multi-faceted capability in not only tactic generation but also premise retrieval. ReProver is based on an encoder-decoder Transformer, ByT5 (Xue et al., 2022), that maps an input string to an output string. |
| Running efficiently on most hardware without GPUs is stated as a design requirement, because Lean users prove theorems on laptops without GPUs; it is a goal, not a measured result. | 2404.12534 — Lean Copilot: Large Language Models as Copilots for Theorem Proving in Lean | 4 | Additionally, theorem proving in Lean can work well on a user's local laptop without GPUs. Lean Copilot needs to avoid adding extra hardware constraints, so it should be able to run efficiently on most hardware even without GPUs. |
| Each model-suggested tactic is first run by a Lean meta-program against the current goals, and candidates that raise errors are dropped before the user sees them. | 2404.12534 — Lean Copilot: Large Language Models as Copilots for Theorem Proving in Lean | 6 | Instead of blindly returning all of them to users, we further check each tactic candidate via a meta-program in Lean that simulates running the tactic candidate on the current proof goals. There are three possible results. If the tactic candidate leads to errors, we drop them from the list. |
| On the Mathematics in Lean theorems, SEARCH_PROOF needed on average 2.08 human-entered tactics versus 3.86 for AESOP and 3.11 for SUGGEST_TACTICS (Table 1 prints 3.10). | 2404.12534 — Lean Copilot: Large Language Models as Copilots for Theorem Proving in Lean | 9 | When used to assist humans, SEARCH_PROOF only requires an average of 2.08 manually-entered tactics, which compares favorably to AESOP (3.86) and SUGGEST_TACTICS (3.11). |
| Averaged per theorem, SEARCH_PROOF automated 74.2% of proof steps versus 58.4% for SUGGEST_TACTICS and 40.1% for AESOP (Table 1 prints 58.3% for SUGGEST_TACTICS). | 2404.12534 — Lean Copilot: Large Language Models as Copilots for Theorem Proving in Lean | 9 | Averaging the percentage over all tested theorems, we find that SEARCH_PROOF can automate about 74.2%of the proof steps in a theorem, significantly more helpful than SUGGEST_TACTICS (58.4%) and AESOP (40.1%). |
| Proving the 168 tactic-style textbook theorems with no human input, AESOP succeeded on 24.4%, SUGGEST_TACTICS on 45.2% and SEARCH_PROOF on 63.7%. | 2404.12534 — Lean Copilot: Large Language Models as Copilots for Theorem Proving in Lean | 10 | \| AESOP \| 3.86 \| 24.4% \| 40.1% \| \| SUGGEST_TACTICS \| 3.10 \| 45.2% \| 58.3% \| \| SEARCH_PROOFS \| 2.08 \| 63.7% \| 74.2% \| |
| The human-assistance numbers come from replaying each theorem's ground-truth tactics one at a time and calling the tools after each, not from a user study, and the AESOP baseline ran with no configured rule set. | 2404.12534 — Lean Copilot: Large Language Models as Copilots for Theorem Proving in Lean | 9 | To mimic a human user, we enter the ground truth tactics one by one. After each tactic, we try to prove the remaining goals using each of the automated tools: SEARCH_PROOF, AESOP, and SUGGEST_TACTICS. For AESOP, we use it out of the box, without manually configuring the rule set. |
| The authors warn that a copilot backed by a static model soon becomes less helpful in a fast-moving project that keeps adding premises, unless the model is updated. | 2404.12534 — Lean Copilot: Large Language Models as Copilots for Theorem Proving in Lean | 26 | Especially in fast-developing formalization projects, new premises can appear frequently. If the knowledge base of the LLM behind the proof automation tools is static, it would require the LLM to be updated once in a while. Otherwise it soon becomes less helpful. |

| JetBrains' Full Line Code Completion ships INT4-quantized LLaMA-like models of 100M parameters with a modified BPE tokenizer. | 2405.08704 — Full Line Code Completion: Bringing AI to Desktop | 6 | In summary, for Full Line Code Completion, INT4-quantized LLaMA-like models with 100M parameters were used. The models utilize a modified BPE tokenizer implemented on top of the YouTokenToMe library. |
| The Python model's data totalled slightly more than 50 GB, split 80%/5%/15% into train, validation and test sets. | 2405.08704 — Full Line Code Completion: Bringing AI to Desktop | 4 | For the training of the Python model, the corresponding proportions of the train-validation-test sets of the data were 80%-5%-15%, adding up to slightly more than 50 GB of data. |
| INT4 quantization in llama.cpp shrank the model from almost 400 MB to slightly over 100 MB and made CPU inference 2x faster on an M2 Mac and 4x faster on an Intel i9 Linux machine. | 2405.08704 — Full Line Code Completion: Bringing AI to Desktop | 6 | Firstly, the size of the model decreased from almost 400 MB to slightly more than 100 MB, which fits within the desired restrictions. Secondly, llama.cpp allows us to shorten the CPU inference time due to more efficient usage of the registers for INT4 data: e.g., the quantized model runs 2 times faster compared to full precision model on M2 CPU on MacOS and 4 times faster on Intel i9 CPU on Linux. |
| The IDE teams set the feature's memory budget at an upper bound of about 20-40% of the IDE's own memory footprint. | 2405.08704 — Full Line Code Completion: Bringing AI to Desktop | 4 | Insights from the IDE development teams give us a general upper bound of about 20-40% of the IDE memory footprint (might differ between products). |
| Measured online, the average CPU time from invoking completion to showing a suggestion was 150 ms including cache initialisation, and the cache was reused in over 90% of completion events. | 2405.08704 — Full Line Code Completion: Bringing AI to Desktop | 10 | The average time to show for FLCC is 150 ms, which is perfect for the user's visual perception [67]. The result includes cache initialization events, as well as cache reusing. According to our observation, the cache is used in over 90% of completion events, which makes a typical time to show even lower. |
| In A/B tests on early-access users, Python users with the feature wrote 1.3 times as much code through completion (22% vs 17% of written code) as users with standard completion only. | 2405.08704 — Full Line Code Completion: Bringing AI to Desktop | 9 | For Python, the metric bumped 1.3 times (from 17% to 22%) for users with FLCC enabled compared to users with standard code completion only. |
| The Python acceptance rate was about 38% (standard completion about 27%), which the authors say cannot be compared directly because suggestion lengths differ. | 2405.08704 — Full Line Code Completion: Bringing AI to Desktop | 10 | The acceptance rate for Full Line Code Completion for Python was about 38%. For standard completion, the value is about 27%, however, no conclusions can be made from this comparison due to significantly different lengths of suggestions. |
| Filtering out suggestions that fail the IDE's code inspections cost only 1% of valuable suggestions in the 2023.3 PyCharm Pro study, and lets the feature show only valid code. | 2405.08704 — Full Line Code Completion: Bringing AI to Desktop | 8 | Our research for the 2023.3 PyCharm Pro release showed that FLCC benefits from filtering out incorrect code. Only 1% of valuable suggestions are lost, and as a trade-off for such a negligible loss, we establish the distinguishing feature of Full Line Code Completion - suggesting only valid code. |
| At their model sizes the authors found knowledge distillation gave no quality improvement, so they did not use it. | 2405.08704 — Full Line Code Completion: Bringing AI to Desktop | 6 | Unlike in many similar circumstances, we do not employ knowledge distillation [56], because we discovered that for our sizes of the models, this technique does not provide any quality improvement. |
| The online results come only from early-access (EAP) users, who the authors say may not represent the broader IDE user base. | 2405.08704 — Full Line Code Completion: Bringing AI to Desktop | 10 | Although the results were statistically significant, they may not fully represent the broader user base of IntelliJ-based IDEs, as EAP participants typically have a deeper understanding and engagement with the IDE's features |

| RTLFixer was published at the 61st ACM/IEEE Design Automation Conference (DAC 2024). | 2311.16543 — RTLFixer: Automatically Fixing RTL Syntax Errors with Large Language Models | 1 | In 61st ACM/IEEE Design Automation Conference (DAC '24), June 23-27, 2024, San Francisco, CA, USA. |
| On VerilogEval-Human, syntax errors made up 55% of the errors in GPT-3.5-generated Verilog, more than simulation (functional) errors. | 2311.16543 — RTLFixer: Automatically Fixing RTL Syntax Errors with Large Language Models | 4 | The VerilogEval-Human benchmark statistics show that syntax errors constitute a significant 55% of errors in GPT-3.5 generated Verilog code, surpassing simulation errors. |
| RTLFixer uses an off-the-shelf gpt-3.5-turbo-16k-0613 with no fine-tuning, after a rule-based pre-fixer, as a ReAct agent limited to 10 Thought-Action-Observation iterations at temperature 0.4, where actions can call the compiler. | 2311.16543 — RTLFixer: Automatically Fixing RTL Syntax Errors with Large Language Models | 4 | We specifically used gpt-3.5-turbo-16k-0613. A simple rulebased syntax fixer is applied to every LLM-generated verilog code, which avoids simple errors such as misplaced timescale derivatives. In all experiments, we set the sampling temperature to 0.4. For ReAct prompting, we restrict the LLM to a maximum of 10 iterations of Thought-Action-Observation, where Action might involve interactions with the compiler. |
| On the 212-implementation VerilogEval-syntax set with GPT-3.5 and Quartus compiler messages, the compile-error fix rate was 98.5% with ReAct plus RAG versus 79.9% for ReAct alone, and 89.9% versus 58.7% for one-shot prompting with and without RAG (mean of 10 runs). | 2311.16543 — RTLFixer: Automatically Fixing RTL Syntax Errors with Large Language Models | 5 | The results with Quartus compiler in Table 1 show that RAG improves the fix rate by 31.2% (58.7% to 89.9% ) for One-shot and 18.6% (79.9% to 98.5%) when using ReAct. |
| With GPT-3.5 and ReAct, the fix rate was 73.1% (no RAG) and 82.0% (RAG) with the terse open-source iverilog messages versus 79.9% and 98.5% with the more informative commercial Quartus messages; GPT-4 with Quartus reached 92% and 99%. | 2311.16543 — RTLFixer: Automatically Fixing RTL Syntax Errors with Large Language Models | 4 | ReAct \| w/o \| 0.671 \| 0.731 \| 0.799 \| 0.92 \| \| ReAct \| w/ \| - \| 0.820 \| 0.985 \| 0.99 |

| On VerilogEval-Human, fixing only syntax errors raised simulation pass@1 from 26.7% to 36.8%, i.e. the reported 10.1% gain is in absolute percentage points. | 2311.16543 — RTLFixer: Automatically Fixing RTL Syntax Errors with Large Language Models | 4 | With just addressing syntax errors using our approach (shown in the inner circle), the pass rate increases from 26.7% to 36.8%. |
| The RAG database is hand-written human expert guidance keyed by compiler error tags and retrieved by exact match: 7 error categories with 30 entries for iverilog and 11 categories with 45 entries for Quartus. | 2311.16543 — RTLFixer: Automatically Fixing RTL Syntax Errors with Large Language Models | 4 | We collected 7 common error categories with 30 entries for iverilog and 11 common error categories with 45 entries for Qaurtus in total. |
| With GPT-4 and Quartus feedback, ReAct improved on one-shot prompting by only about 1%, which the authors read as GPT-4 fixing syntax errors without needing reasoning, planning or iteration. | 2311.16543 — RTLFixer: Automatically Fixing RTL Syntax Errors with Large Language Models | 6 | When comparing the results of GPT-4 between One-shot and ReAct, we observe minor improvements of approximately 1%, suggesting that GPT-4 is already a robust agent proficient in fixing syntax errors without the need for reasoning, action planning, and iterative refinement. |

| HDLdebugger's ACM reference format places it in the proceedings of KDD 2024 (13 pages). | 2403.11671 — HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models | 1 | 2024. HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models. In Proceedings of (KDD'24). ACM, New York, NY, USA, 13 pages. |
| Training pairs are synthesized by applying LLM-derived modification functions to clean industrial HDL and compiling the results to capture real error messages, so the faults and fixes are known by construction and no manual diagnosis is needed. | 2403.11671 — HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models | 3 | Given that our instructions systematically brought the errors, we can employ reverse engineering to identify the faults and produce solutions directly. This bypasses the need for manual error diagnosis |
| The data are 92,143 generated (buggy code, compiler error messages, correct code) instances from Huawei HDL files, split 8:2 into train and test, so the test set comes from the same mutation generator as the training set. | 2403.11671 — HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models | 5 | Based on these HDL code files, we use the data generation in Sec. 2.1 to generate 92,143 distinct HDL training code instances. Each HDL training code instance consists of the buggy HDL code, error messages, and the correct HDL code. Specifically, in the experiments, we split the data into training and testing sets at a ratio of 8:2, respectively. |
| HDLdebugger fine-tunes CodeLlama-13b, a size the authors chose to balance training and deployment cost against performance. | 2403.11671 — HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models | 6 | Specifically, we selected CodeLlama-13b as our base model from the available code LLMs. |
| On the held-out synthetic Huawei test split, HDLdebugger reached an 81.93% pass rate versus 71.57% for the strongest of its 13 baselines, WizardCoder fine-tuned on the same data. | 2403.11671 — HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models | 6 | WizardCoder w/ SFT \| 71.57% \| 1.04 \| 1.06 \| \| HDLdebugger(ours) \| 81.93% \| 1.00 \| 1.00 |
| The ChatGPT and GPT-4 baselines (3.01% and 6.35% pass rate) were run with certain code specifications omitted for privacy, so they did not see the same inputs as the other systems. | 2403.11671 — HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models | 6 | Due to privacy concerns, certain code specifications have been omitted for testing purposes when using ChatGPT or GPT4. |
| In the CodeLlama ablation on the same test split, direct prompting scored 4.01%, adding RAG 15.05%, SFT 70.56% and SFT with generated thoughts 74.91%, versus 81.93% for the full RAG plus SFT-with-thoughts system. | 2403.11671 — HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models | 7 | Direct(CodeLlama) \| 4.01% \| 2.26 \| 35.51 \| \| + RAG \| 15.05% \| 2.20 \| 5.34 \| \| + SFT \| 70.56% \| 1.19 \| 1.19 \| \| + SFT w/ th \| 74.91% \| 1.09 \| 1.13 |
| The Verilog-specialized VeriGen scored under 1% raw and 1.34% with RAG on Huawei's HDL, but 67.55% after supervised fine-tuning on the synthetic pairs. | 2403.11671 — HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models | 6 | VeriGen \| <1% \| 2.46 \| 49.16 \| \| VeriGen w/ RAG \| 1.34% \| 2.56 \| 37.12 \| \| VeriGen w/ SFT \| 67.55% \| 1.35 \| 1.38 |
| The explanatory thoughts used as fine-tuning targets are written by an LLM shown both the buggy and the corrected code, sampled several times, and the one whose resulting fix has the smallest edit distance to the ground truth is kept. | 2403.11671 — HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models | 12 | The core principle of our approach involves submitting both the buggy code and its corrected version to the LLM, subsequently prompting the model to generate instructive guidance. |
| In deployment the authors found compile-passing fixes insufficient, since compiling code may still hurt chip performance and engineers often want hints rather than exact rewrites, for which they have no metric. | 2403.11671 — HDLdebugger: Streamlining HDL debugging with Large Language Models | 13 | Code that successfully compiles may not necessarily optimize chip design performance. Additionally, exact solutions are not always required; engineers often benefit from suggestive "hints" that inspire solutions to complex issues. |

| Break-It-Fix-It (Yasunaga and Liang) was published at ICML 2021 (PMLR 139). | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 1 | Proceedings of the 38 th International Conference on Machine Learning, PMLR 139, 2021. |
| BIFI learns a fixer from unpaired code plus a critic (the Python AST parser or the C compiler) by keeping only fixer outputs on real bad code that the critic accepts, and by training a breaker on those real pairs to generate realistic bad code, instead of relying only on heuristic corruption. | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 1 | (i) we use the critic to check a fixer's output on real bad inputs and add good (fixed) outputs to the training data, and (ii) we train a breaker to generate realistic bad code from good code. |
| Without any labelled data, BIFI reaches 90.5% repair accuracy on GitHub-Python (+28.5 points absolute over its initial fixer trained on random perturbations, 62.0%) and 71.7% on DeepFix (+5.6 points over DrRepair, 66.1%, on BIFI's 20% held-out split). | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 1 | BIFI outperforms state-of-the-art methods, obtaining 90.5% repair accuracy on GitHubPython (+28.5%) and 71.7% on DeepFix (+5.6%). Notably, BIFI does not require any labeled data |
| GitHub-Python is 3M Python 3 snippets of 10-128 tokens labelled only by the AST parser as critic: 38K are bad, of which 15K real bad snippets are the test set and the remaining 23K are the only real bad examples BIFI trains on. | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 5 | Using this critic, we obtain 38K snippets of bad code and 3M snippets of good code. From the 38K bad examples, we holdout 15K as the final test set, and make the remaining 23K bad examples available for BIFI. |
| On GitHub-Python a repair counts as successful when the output parses and lies within a Levenshtein distance of under 5 tokens from the input; the authors note that semantic preservation is not checked automatically, so the edit-distance bound stands in for it. | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 2 | We acknowledge that while we want f(x) to be semantics-preserving, it is nontrivial to ensure this automatically, so we rely on the edit distance. |
| On GitHub-Python the BIFI fixer and breaker are each a small encoder-decoder Transformer with 4 layers, 8 attention heads and hidden size 256, trained on one GTX Titan X GPU; the paper gives no parameter count or inference speed. | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 5 | (Vaswani et al., 2017) with 4 layers, 8 attention heads, and hidden states of size 256. The model parameters are optimized by Adam |
| BIFI's initial GitHub-Python fixer is trained purely on synthetic corruption: 1-3 tokens dropped, inserted or replaced uniformly, applied 8 times to each of 2.6M good snippets. | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 5 | which drops, inserts, or replaces 1-3 tokens in code with uniform distribution. We apply bsynthetic 8 times to each of the 2.6M good code snippets to prepare the initial training data Psynthetic. |
| Training the initial fixer for as many extra rounds on synthetic perturbations only gave no improvement (62.7% versus 62.0% on GitHub-Python), which the authors read as a performance ceiling for synthetic-only training. | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 6 | however, this did not provide an improvement, suggesting that there is a performance ceiling if we only train on synthetic data. |
| With only 10% of the 23K real bad GitHub-Python examples available, critic-verified self-training (FixerOnly) still reaches 78.5% against 62.7% for continued synthetic-only training. | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 7 | making FixerOnly perform better than using synthetic data alone (i.e., 78.5% vs 62.7%). |
| On GitHub-Python, removing the critic's verification drops BIFI round 2 from 90.5% to 84.0%, and removing both the critic and training on real bad code (plain backtranslation) drops it to 80.1%. | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 6 | \| - critic \| Round-2 \| 84.0% \| \| - both (backtranslation) \| Round-2 \| 80.1% \| |
| Error categories that random perturbation rarely produces are where synthetic training fails: on GitHub-Python indentation errors the initial fixer repairs about 39%, rising to about 85% after BIFI rounds 1-2. | 2106.06600 — Break-It-Fix-It: Unsupervised Learning for Program Repair | 7 | but it achieves significantly higher performance in round 1, 2 (e.g., 39%→85% for indentation errors). |
| DrRepair (Yasunaga and Liang, graph-based self-supervised repair from diagnostic feedback) was published at ICML 2020 (PMLR 119). | 2005.10636 — Graph-based, Self-Supervised Program Repair from Diagnostic Feedback | 1 | Proceedings of the 37 th International Conference on Machine Learning, Vienna, Austria, PMLR 119, 2020. |
| DrRepair reports a 68.2% full repair rate on the DeepFix raw test set (+22.9 points over the prior best, SampleFix at 45.3%) and a 48.4% synthesis success rate on SPoC (+3.7 points), although the SPoC table in this version does not list a 48.4% entry for DrRepair. | 2005.10636 — Graph-based, Self-Supervised Program Repair from Diagnostic Feedback | 1 | 68.2% full repair rate on DeepFix (+22.9% over the prior best), and 48.4% synthesis success rate on SPoC (+3.7% over the prior best). |
| DrRepair's program-feedback graph takes every identifier in the code and every symbol quoted in the compiler message as nodes and links identical symbols, so graph attention can jump from the message to the declaration that caused it. | 2005.10636 — Graph-based, Self-Supervised Program Repair from Diagnostic Feedback | 2 | it takes all identifiers (e.g. a, b) in the source code and any symbols in the diagnostic arguments (e.g. a, char) as nodes, and connects instances of the same symbols with edges to encode the semantic correspondence |
| DrRepair is a small LSTM-plus-graph-attention model (bidirectional LSTM encoders, 2 graph-attention layers, a pointer-generator decoder) with state size 200 and 200-dimensional token embeddings, trained on one GTX Titan X; no parameter count or inference speed is given. | 2005.10636 — Graph-based, Self-Supervised Program Repair from Diagnostic Feedback | 6 | We set the dimension of input token embeddings and position embeddings to be 200 and 100. The LSTMs and graph attention networks have a state size of 200. |
| DrRepair conditions on only the first compiler message and must predict which line to edit itself, because the line the compiler reports is often not the line that needs the fix. | 2005.10636 — Graph-based, Self-Supervised Program Repair from Diagnostic Feedback | 2 | If the compiler returns multiple error messages, we use only the first one. |
| For self-supervised pre-training, DrRepair corrupts 310K compiling Codeforces C++ programs about 50 times each and keeps the variants that fail to compile, yielding about 1.5M (broken code, compiler feedback, fix) examples with no human labels. | 2005.10636 — Graph-based, Self-Supervised Program Repair from Diagnostic Feedback | 5 | For each program, we then create roughly 50 corrupted versions by applying DrPerturb and keeping ones that fail to compile. This yields ∼1.5M extra training examples |
| The corruption is not random token noise but DrPerturb: 1-5 heuristic edits from four modules (syntax, identifier type, identifier typo, keyword), sampled with probabilities 0.3/0.5/0.15/0.05 to match compiler-error statistics. | 2005.10636 — Graph-based, Self-Supervised Program Repair from Diagnostic Feedback | 5 | samples 1-5 modules fromM (with replacement) and applies them to an input program sequentially. We sample each module with probability 0.3, 0.5, 0.15, 0.05, respectively, motivated by the distribution of errors found in our analysis (Table 1). |
| Without the compiler message as input, the base model scores about the same on synthetic dev data but only 34.0% full repair on the real DeepFix test set, against 62.5% with the message: it learns superficial patterns of the synthetic data. | 2005.10636 — Graph-based, Self-Supervised Program Repair from Diagnostic Feedback | 6 | We find that while it attains comparable scores to "our base" on the synthetic dev, the performance drops a lot on the raw test set: 34.0% acc., similar to the prior work (30-40% acc.). |
| The same base model trained on DrPerturb data repairs 62.5% of the DeepFix test set, against 49.4% when trained on random token dropout, although random dropout produces more distinct compiler-error types (170 versus 156). | 2005.10636 — Graph-based, Self-Supervised Program Repair from Diagnostic Feedback | 7 | while Random produces more distinct types of compiler errors than DrPerturb in terms of the number (170 vs 156), models trained by DrPerturb outperform Random by more than 10% |
| On DeepFix full repair, the program-feedback graph adds about 3 points (62.5% to 66.4%) and self-supervised pre-training about 2 more (to 68.2%). | 2005.10636 — Graph-based, Self-Supervised Program Repair from Diagnostic Feedback | 7 | provides a 3% boost over "base" in full repair rate, and self-supervised pre-training ("base + graph + pre-train") provides a further improvement of 2% |

| SynShine (the arXiv 2104.14671 version) is a three-model pipeline for Java syntax errors: BLOCKFIX repairs brace nesting, javac then localises the faulty line, LINEFIX edits that line given the javac message, and UNKFIX fills identifiers with a masked language model. | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 3 | The second stage (LINEFIX) in SYNSHINE uses the line-location of the error, as detected by the standard javac compiler, together with the actual error message, and generates relevant fixes for delimiters, operators, and keywords |
| LINEFIX and UNKFIX build on a RoBERTa-base-sized model: 12 layers, hidden size 768, 12 heads, with a byte-level BPE vocabulary capped at 25K subtokens. | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 5 | Our RoBERTa architecture consists of 12 attention layers, 768 hidden dimensions, and 12 self-attention heads in each layer. We applied Byte Level BPE (Byte Pair Encoding) tokenizer [23] limiting the subtoken vocabulary size to 25K. |
| SynShine's masked-language-model pre-training uses 1.2 billion tokens from the 5000 most-starred Java projects on GitHub. | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 5 | we collected 5000 most starred Java projects from GitHub (since our end-goal is to correct Java syntax errors). We tokenized the files, yielding 1.2 billion tokens for the pre-training. |
| Fine-tuning is supervised on real novice error/fix pairs from the Blackbox dataset (1.7M pairs in total); after holding out 100K test samples and dropping brace-imbalanced programs, about 540K examples train LINEFIX. | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 6 | We discarded the programs with imbalanced curly braces from the training set, and after tokenization, we found around 540K examples to train the model. |
| Instead of autoregressive generation, LINEFIX classifies: one multi-label head picks token positions and another picks among 154 edit commands (insert, delete or substitute a delimiter, keyword, operator or the placeholder unk). | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 3 | LINEFIX outputs one of 154 possible editing commands, to insert/delete/substitute delimiters, keywords, operators, or identifiers. We limit its output vocabulary to 154. |
| On a random sample of 100,000 Blackbox single-token-error programs up to 1000 tokens, scored by top-1 exact match with the human fix, SynShine reaches 74.89% against 56.91% for BF+FF, 63.25% for DeepFix (short) and 56.89% for SequenceR. | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 8 | \| 46.00% \| 63.25% \| 62.14% \| 56.89% \| 56.91% \| 74.89% \| |
| On programs needing 2 or 3 edits, SynShine's accuracy falls to 29.4% and 14.4% (single-token errors are 57% of Blackbox). | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 9 | We also applied our model on files that required 2 and 3 edits to fix the program and observed 29.4% and 14.4% accuracy, which is much higher than the reported accuracy by Ahmed et al. (19% and 9%). |
| On 10,000 javac-flagged lines, adding the javac message to LINEFIX's input raises complete correction from 86.71% to 89.39% and localisation F-score from 90.75% to 93.58%; the authors stress that javac's line localisation, not the message, is the large win. | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 10 | \| No \| 90.75% \| 92.41% \| 86.71% \| \| Yes \| 93.58% \| 93.18% \| 89.39% \| |
| In a sample of 50 programs, the javac message alone was not that helpful in roughly 60% of cases, and the model learned to ignore or reinterpret misleading messages rather than translate them into fixes. | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 10 | We observed that in roughly 60% cases, the javac warning is not that helpful, and the model learns to respond in fairly nuanced ways to address the error. |
| Integrated into VS Code as a CPU-only local correction server, SynShine answered 200 randomly chosen erroneous programs in 0.88 s on average (standard deviation 0.49 s, maximum 2.2 s). | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 7 | The average response time is 0.88 seconds (standard deviation 0.49s, maximum 2.2s). |
| The SynShine correction server ran on an AMD Ryzen 7 2700X CPU and occupied 1.765 GB of memory. | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 7 | The CPU we used for the experiment is "AMD Ryzen 7 2700X". The code correction server occupies 1.765 GB of the memory. |
| Exact match with the human fix undercounts: 13% to 45% of SynShine's failures (depending on program length) still compile, but in a manual sample of 50 such cases only about 18% were semantically correct. | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 11 | We randomly collect 50 cases where the model generates a compilable fix, that fails to match the user fix recorded in Blackbox. We found that about 18% of programs are semantically correct. |
| SynShine's accuracy falls with program length, from 82% for 1-100 tokens to 55% for 901-1000 tokens, and on the shortest programs DeepFix (short) at 77% is close behind. | 2104.14671 — SYNFIX: Automatically Fixing Syntax Errors using Compiler Diagnostics | 9 | Our overall accuracy ranges between 55% to 82%, and always outperforms DeepFix long (18%-74%), and short (9%-77%), SequenceR (54%-59%) and BF+FF (29%-65%). |
| DeepBugs frames name-based bug detection as binary classification and needs no human-labelled bugs: negative examples come from simple AST transformations (swapping arguments, replacing an operator or an operand) applied to presumed-correct code. | 1805.11683 — DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-based Bug Detection | 1 | To address the challenge that effectively learning a bug detector requires examples of both correct and incorrect code, we create likely incorrect code examples from an existing corpus of code through simple code transformations. |
| Each DeepBugs detector is a tiny feedforward network with a single hidden layer of 200 units over concatenated Word2Vec (CBOW, 200-dimensional) identifier embeddings. | 1805.11683 — DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-based Bug Detection | 8 | a single hidden layer of size 200, and an output layer with a single element that represents the probability computed by D. |
| DeepBugs trains on 100,000 deduplicated JavaScript files and evaluates on another 50,000 (68.6 million lines of code in total). | 1805.11683 — DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-based Bug Detection | 11 | In total, the corpus contains 68.6 million lines of code. We use 100,000 files for training and the remaining 50,000 files for validation. |
| The 89.06%-94.70% accuracy figures for DeepBugs' three detectors are measured on artificially seeded bugs in the 50,000 validation files, not on real bugs. | 1805.11683 — DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-based Bug Detection | 15 | The accuracy ranges between 89.06% and 94.70%, i.e., all bug detectors are highly effective at distinguishing correct from incorrect code examples. |
| On real code, the authors manually inspected the top 50 warnings of each of the three detectors: 95 of the 150 were bugs and 7 code-quality problems, so 68% pointed to a real problem. | 1805.11683 — DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-based Bug Detection | 12 | Out of the 150 inspected warnings, 95 warnings point to bugs and 7 warnings point to a code quality problem, i.e., 68% of all warnings point to an actual problem. |
| Precision on real code varies a lot by detector: the swapped-arguments detector's top 50 warnings held 23 bugs and 27 false positives. | 1805.11683 — DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-based Bug Detection | 13 | \| Swapped arguments \| 50 \| 23 \| 0 \| 27 \| |
| A trained DeepBugs detector takes on average under 20 ms per JavaScript file to predict, and training plus prediction over all 150,000 files takes 36-73 minutes per detector. | 1805.11683 — DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-based Bug Detection | 16 | Running both training and prediction on all 150,000 files takes between 36 minutes and 73 minutes per bug detector. The average prediction time per JavaScript file is below 20 milliseconds. |
| Learned embeddings add only modestly over random identifier vectors: accuracy on seeded bugs goes from 93.88% to 94.70% (swapped arguments), 89.15% to 92.21% (binary operator) and 84.79% to 89.06% (binary operand). | 1805.11683 — DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-based Bug Detection | 15 | \| Swapped arguments \| 93.88% \| 94.70% \| \| Wrong binary operator \| 89.15% \| 92.21% \| \| Wrong binary operand \| 84.79% \| 89.06% \| |
| At the default probability threshold of 0.5 the three detectors emit 116,941 warnings on the validation files, about one per 196 lines, so in practice only top-ranked warnings are usable. | 1805.11683 — DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-based Bug Detection | 15 | For a threshold of t = 0.5, the three bug detectors report a total of 116,941 warnings, which corresponds to roughly one warning per 196 lines of code. |
| The authors name an inherent limitation: name-based detection fails when developers use unconventional names, which is the main source of DeepBugs' false positives. | 1805.11683 — DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-based Bug Detection | 14 | These false positives illustrate an inherent limitation of name-based bug detection: Because the approach relies in reasonable names that are in line with common practice, it may fail when developers choose to diverge from the norm. |
| Ray et al. study 266 snapshots of 10 Java projects (202.6M non-comment lines, 38,003 commits), of which 8,296 commits are classified as bug fixes. | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 3 | These snapshots contain 38,003 distinct commits, of which 8,296 were marked as bug fixing changes |
| Bug-fix commits, and therefore buggy lines, are labelled automatically from commit-message keywords rather than by humans, which the authors list as a source of false positives and false negatives. | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 4 | We mark a commit as bugfix, if the corresponding commit log contains at least one of the error related keywords |
| The model is a cache n-gram language model: a global n-gram model interpolated with a cache built on the current file, with maximum n-gram order 10 and minimum back-off order 4, scoring each line's average token cross-entropy. | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 5 | We follow Tu et al. [36] to set the maximum order of cache ngrams to 10. |
| Buggy lines are more entropic than non-buggy lines, but the effect shrinks with fix size: 1.67-1.80 bits higher (Cohen's d 0.56) for commits deleting at most 2 lines, and only 0.80-0.84 bits (d 0.26) at up to 30 deleted lines. | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 6 | for a max\_delete size of 2, the entropies of buggy lines are on average 1.67 to 1.80 bits higher, with a high effect size of 0.56. |
| For commits deleting at most 5 lines, the entropy of buggy code drops by 1.19-1.26 bits once it is fixed (95% confidence). | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 6 | On average, entropy of the buggy lines drop after the bugfixes, with a drop of 1.19 to 1.26 bit, with 95% confidence. |
| Raw cache-LM entropy is a weak line ranker at small inspection budgets: only 17% better than random at 5% of lines inspected; it needs a syntax-type normalisation and a bug-history weight ($gram+wType) to exceed twice random. | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 7 | Indeed, average performance of $gramwas significantly better than random at 20% (nearly twice as good) but only marginally so at 5% (17% better than random). |
| On Rahman et al.'s Apache release data, the entropy-based finder and the static bug finders PMD and FindBugs perform comparably; the slight edge for entropy vanishes under full credit or without the bug-size threshold. | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 8 | SBF and NBF performed comparably; NBF performed slightly better when using both partial credit and the specified threshold for bug-sizes, but when dropping the threshold, and/or with full credit, no significant difference remains between NBF and SBF. |
| Ordering static-bug-finder warnings by tool priority and then by entropy gives the most cost-effective inspection order the paper tests. | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 9 | Result 5: Ordering SBF warnings by priority and entropy significantly improves SBF performance. |
| Naturalness misses bugs that look familiar: a copy-paste error reusing an existing statement had low entropy, and its correct fix raised entropy by 5.75 bits. | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 7 | Since, the statement related to maxChunkSize was already present in the existing corpus, the line was not surprising. Hence, its entropy was low although it was a bug. |
| The authors concede that a surprising line is less informative than a static-analysis warning, since entropy only flags a line as surprising while a static bug finder says what is wrong. | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 9 | NBF just marks a line as "surprising", whereas SBF provides specific warnings. |
| As a sanity check on 3 C/C++ projects (2,518 bug-fix commits), buggy lines were 0.87-1.16 bits more entropic than non-buggy lines at a 15-line threshold. | 1506.01159 — On the "Naturalness" of Buggy Code | 9 | we found that buggy lines were between 0.87 and 1.16 bits more entropic than non-buggy llines when using a threshold of 15 lines |

| AutoCommenter (Google) was published at the 1st ACM International Conference on AI-Powered Software, AIware 2024. | 2405.13565 — AI-Assisted Assessment of Coding Practices in Modern Code Review | 1 | In Proceedings of the 1st ACM International Conference on AI-Powered Software (AIware '24), July 15-16, 2024, Porto de Galinhas, Brazil. |
| AutoCommenter is one task of a multi-task T5X model (size not disclosed, 2048-token context) trained on over 3 billion examples, of which about 800k are best-practice comments mined from human code reviews that cite a style-guide URL. | 2405.13565 — AI-Assisted Assessment of Coding Practices in Modern Code Review | 3 | The training corpus consists of over 3 billion examples, of which the best practice analysis dataset contributes about 800k examples. |
| A single confidence threshold of 0.98 suppressed many correct predictions (about 80% of several hundred sampled below-threshold predictions were correct), and per-language thresholds also failed, so AutoCommenter moved to per-URL thresholds set from validation data. | 2405.13565 — AI-Assisted Assessment of Coding Practices in Modern Code Review | 5 | We manually sampled several hundred results and observed that around 80% of predictions below the threshold were still correct-that is, the false-negative rate was very high at t = 0.98. Additionally, we observed that predictions in Python showed a significantly different distribution of confidence scores, which were disproportionately impacted by thresholding. We conjecture that the training dataset composition (number of distinct URLs and URL frequencies) and specificity of the best practice documents are reasons, but leave a deeper investigation to future work. An attempt to deploy per-language thresholds proved ineffective as a single threshold per language still did not adequately capture the model's ability to correctly predict hundreds of diverse best practices. This led to a lack of diversity in predicted URLs as the model tended to produce higher scores for some URLs vs. others, irrespective of correctness. These observations led to the first major change to AutoCommenter: per-URL thresholds computed based on the intrinsic evaluation on the validation dataset. |
| Beam search (n=4) tripled posting frequency but had a 2-second median latency, too slow for the IDE, so AutoCommenter uses beam search in code review and greedy decoding for sub-second IDE diagnostics. | 2405.13565 — AI-Assisted Assessment of Coding Practices in Modern Code Review | 5 | While beam search increased the posting frequency and diversity, inference became noticeably slower (median latency of 2 seconds). Given that this latency is prohibitive for interactive use in the IDE, we ultimately decided to use beam search for the code review system and greedy search for the IDE. |
| After several months with about 3 thousand early adopters the developer-feedback useful ratio plateaued at 54%, and 15 independent raters scored the same ~370 comments 60% useful, both below the 80% target set for wider deployment. | 2405.13565 — AI-Assisted Assessment of Coding Practices in Modern Code Review | 6 | The useful ratio from the rater evaluation was 60%, slightly higher than the 54% from the developer feedback on the same comments, but well below our target of 80% for wider deployment. |
| The useful ratio reached the 80% target not through thresholds but by suppressing 17 (then 5 more) non-actionable best-practice URLs, which raised it from 54% to 66% on historical developer feedback, and by manually rewriting the summaries of frequently posted URLs. | 2405.13565 — AI-Assisted Assessment of Coding Practices in Modern Code Review | 6 | First, the rater study identified 17 non-actionable URLs, whose suppression increased the historical useful ratio from 54% to 66% on developer feedback, and from 60% to 74% on rater feedback. We further analyzed comments linked to similar, unrated URLs and suppressed an additional 5. Second, we reviewed and manually updated summaries for all frequently posted URLs. Together, these changes were sufficient to reach our target useful ratio of 80% for the next stage of deployment. |
| When a Python import best practice changed in 2022 but the training data predated it, the model kept posting outdated advice, and AutoCommenter suppressed those predictions with regular-expression filters rather than wait for a retrain. | 2405.13565 — AI-Assisted Assessment of Coding Practices in Modern Code Review | 6 | We opted for suppression of specific best-practice predictions, using conditional filtering (matching regular expressions on the source code) for two reasons. First, it can be dynamically deployed and immediately applied. |
| In a July 2023 A/B experiment over about half of Google's developers, AutoCommenter produced no statistically significant change in code-review duration, active review time or review iterations, only a slight coding-speed improvement. | 2405.13565 — AI-Assisted Assessment of Coding Practices in Modern Code Review | 6 | We did not detect any statistically significant change in any of the following: total duration of code reviews, time developers actively spent on the code review, the number of comment-response iterations between the author and the reviewer. |
| About 40% of AutoCommenter comments are estimated to be resolved: the comment disappeared in 50% of 6000 snapshot pairs, and in a manual sample of 40 such pairs 80% were direct fixes by the author. | 2405.13565 — AI-Assisted Assessment of Coding Practices in Modern Code Review | 7 | We found that in 80% of cases, a change made by the author directly resolved the issue described by the posted comment. Therefore, we estimate that the commentresolution rate is about 40% |
| Of the 50 most frequently predicted best-practice URLs, three of the authors judged 33 (66%) beyond what a traditional linter detects or could easily be built to detect. | 2405.13565 — AI-Assisted Assessment of Coding Practices in Modern Code Review | 8 | For 33/50 (66%) of these best practices, violation detection is beyond the scope of traditional static analysis. |
| Eiffel-tools targets Eiffel as a low-resource language with little public code but a built-in specification language and a static verifier, AutoProof. | 2609.03086 — Large Language Models and Language Server Protocol: a match made in context | 2 | Eiffel is a low-resource language; it has a first-class support for specifications and a static verifier AutoProof, but the low amount of public code makes LLM code generation more challenging. |
| Eiffel-tools, an LSP language server, runs AutoProof with timeouts on every LLM suggestion and reverts LLM-generated code to its last stable state when verification fails. | 2609.03086 — Large Language Models and Language Server Protocol: a match made in context | 5 | Eiffel-tools uses AutoProof to verify existing code or LLM-generated suggestions. Because AutoProof may not terminate on some inputs, Eiffel-tools runs it with timeouts: any verification exceeding a time limit is killed, and if the code was LLM-generated, the tool reverts it to its previous stable state upon failure. |
| The interactive fix-feature-body code action loops LLM candidate, AutoProof, retry, and applies no edit to the user's code unless a candidate verifies. | 2609.03086 — Large Language Models and Language Server Protocol: a match made in context | 6 | If a candidate body passes verification, Eiffel-tools copies that body into the original feature; otherwise, the loop continues until a candidate passes or a maximum number of attempts is reached. Unless a candidate succeeds, no changes are applied to the original feature. |
| For LLM-suggested contracts, the server parses each candidate and drops contracts that are redundant, not usable by the verifier, reference invalid identifiers, or call with the wrong number of arguments, before inserting the rest. | 2609.03086 — Large Language Models and Language Server Protocol: a match made in context | 6 | Are redundant - Are not model-based, and thus unusable by AutoProof - Contain invalid top-level identifiers - Contain calls with the wrong number of arguments |

More importantly, even at its peak, it exhibits a significant deployment gap compared to our FP16 approach. It achieves a maximum training accuracy of only 95% (vs. 99% in FP16) and a score of 34% (vs. 39% in FP16) on the AIME 2024 benchmark, demonstrating a clear performance ceiling. |

| AceReason is strictly on-policy (a single gradient update per group of rollouts) and sets the KL coefficient beta to 0, so its GRPO objective reduces to REINFORCE with group-normalized rewards. | 2505.16400 — AceReason-Nemotron: Advancing Math and Code Reasoning through Reinforcement Learning | 5 | Our experiments strictly adhere to on-policy training by performing a single gradient update after generating a group of 𝐺 rollouts. This approach ensures stable RL training and helps prevent entropy collapse. Consequently, the policy used for data collection matches the current policy, i.e., 𝜋𝜃old (· \| 𝑞) = 𝜋𝜃(· \| 𝑞), and the importance weight 𝑟𝑖,𝑡(𝜃) = 1. Additionally, we eliminate the KL divergence term by setting 𝛽 = 0, thus the GRPO objective becames REINFORCE objective (Williams, 1992) with group-normalized rewards, |

(2025) |

| With a 64-worker process pool, math verification took about 3.9 s per 1,024 instances and code verification (binary: pass all tests within the time limit) about 552.4 s per 1,024 instances, roughly 142 times slower (552.4 / 3.9). | 2505.16400 — AceReason-Nemotron: Advancing Math and Code Reasoning through Reinforcement Learning | 5 | Using a process pool with 64 workers, the average verification time is approximately 3.9 seconds per 1,024 instances. - For coding problem verification, we utilize a local sandbox verifier, following the code execution tools implemented in the LiveCodeBench repository (Jain et al., 2024). Given the model's output, we extract the code generated within '''python[code]''' code block that follows &lt;\think&gt; token. Binary rewards are then assigned based on code execution outcome on full set of test cases. A positive reward will be granted if and only if the extracted code successfully passes all test cases within the specific time limit. Using a process pool with 64 workers, the average verification time for code is approximately 552.4 seconds per 1,024 instances. |

For instance, on the AIME 2024 benchmark, using only correct data resulted in a 4.78% performance improvement compared to training with mixed responses. This suggests that when the underlying reasoning is relatively straightforward and aligns well with the model's prior capabilities, exposure to incorrect paths acts primarily as detrimental noise, interfering with the model's grasp of established, correct patterns. | For 5151 Codeforces and 2504 AtCoder problems without public tests, DeepSeek-V2.5 wrote Python test generators; tests that made correct submissions fail were dropped, and subsets that caught incorrect submissions were kept. | 2501.12948 — DeepSeek-R1: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability in LLMs via Reinforcement Learning | 21 | For code data, we collect a large set of competitive programming problems. In detail, We have compiled an extensive collection of competitive programming problems from multiple online judge (OJ) platforms, specifically 5151 problems from Codeforces and 2504 problems from AtCoder. Since the original test cases are not publicly available from these platforms, we developed a methodology to create reliable test cases for each problem. Our approach involves using DeepSeek-V2.5 to generate candidate test cases, followed by a rigorous validation process. Specifically, we prompted DeepSeek-V2.5 to write Python programs that generate test cases tailored to each problem's requirements as shown in Listing 2. After obtaining numerous candidate test cases, we implemented a two-phase filtering procedure. First, we used correct submissions to eliminate invalid test cases that produced incorrect outputs. Then, we strategically selected subsets of test cases that successfully identified flaws in incorrect submissions. |
| R1's code RL data held 17k algorithm problems plus 8k bug-fixing problems from real GitHub issues, each with an issue, the buggy source and unit tests that partially or completely fail. | 2501.12948 — DeepSeek-R1: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability in LLMs via Reinforcement Learning | 19 | Coding dataset includes 17k algorithm competition questions, along with 8k bug fixing problems. The algorithm competition questions are similar to problems found on platforms like Codeforces or LeetCode. Each problem typically includes a detailed problem description, constraints, and multiple input-output examples. The task is to write a complete function or program that can solve the problem correctly and efficiently, passing a comprehensive set of hidden test cases that assess both correctness and performance. These problems test algorithmic skills, including dynamic programming, graph theory, string manipulation, and data structure usage. The bug-fixing problems are extracted from real-world GitHub issues. Each task provides an issue description, a buggy version of the source code, and a set of unit tests that partially or completely fail. |

| Qwen3's distillation-versus-RL comparison started both arms from the same off-policy distilled 8B checkpoint, used only math and code queries, and found distillation significantly better at approximately 1/10 of the GPU hours. | 2505.09388 — Qwen3 Technical Report | 20 | both starting from the same off-policy distilled 8B checkpoint. The results, summarized in Table 21, show that distillation achieves significantly better performance than reinforcement learning while requiring approximately only 1/10 of the GPU hours. |
| Qwen3's strong-to-weak distillation pipeline built five dense models (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B, 8B and 14B) and the Qwen3-30B-A3B MoE, so it covers the 0.6B-4B models and more. | 2505.09388 — Qwen3 Technical Report | 12 | The Strong-to-Weak Distillation pipeline is specifically designed to optimize lightweight models, encompassing 5 dense models (Qwen3-0.6B, 1.7B, 4B, 8B, and 14B) and one MoE model (Qwen3-30B-A3B). |
| In Qwen3's preliminary experiments, distilling teacher logits into small students replaced running the four-stage pipeline per small model, gave higher Pass@1 and Pass@64, and needed only 1/10 of the GPU hours of the four-stage method. | 2505.09388 — Qwen3 Technical Report | 10 | This approach eliminates the necessity of performing an exhaustive four-stage training process individually for every small-scale model. It leads to better immediate performance, as indicated by higher Pass@1 scores, and also improves the model's ability of exploration, as reflected in improved Pass@64 results. In addition, it achieves these gains with much greater training efficiency, requiring only 1/10 of the GPU hours compared to the four-stage training method. |
| In the Qwen3-8B comparison, on-policy distillation from teacher logits raised pass@64 on AIME'24 and AIME'25 over the starting checkpoint, while RL did not improve pass@64 at all, only pass@1. | 2505.09388 — Qwen3 Technical Report | 20 | Furthermore, distillation from teacher logits enables the student model to expand its exploration space and enhance its reasoning potential, as evidenced by the improved pass@64 scores on the AIME'24 and AIME'25 benchmarks after distillation, compared to the initial checkpoint. In contrast, reinforcement learning does not lead to any improvement in pass@64 scores. |

| Qwen3's reasoning-RL pool was 3,995 query-verifier pairs chosen to be unused in cold start, learnable for the cold-start model, as challenging as possible and broad across sub-domains, trained with GRPO. | 2505.09388 — Qwen3 Technical Report | 10 | The query-verifier pairs used in the Reasoning RL stage must satisfy the following four criteria: (1) They were not used during the cold-start phase. (2) They are learnable for the cold-start model. (3) They are as challenging as possible. (4) They cover a broad range of sub-domains. We ultimately collect a total of 3,995 query-verifier pairs, and employed GRPO (Shao et al., 2024) to update the model parameters. |
| On Qwen3-32B, the thinking-mode-fusion SFT and general RL stages that followed reasoning RL lowered thinking-mode AIME'24 and LiveCodeBench; the authors attribute it to training on a broader range of general tasks. | 2505.09388 — Qwen3 Technical Report | 22 | In contrast, for challenging tasks like AIME'24 and LiveCodeBench, the performance in thinking mode actually decreases after these two training stages. We conjecture this degradation is due to the model being trained on a broader range of general tasks, which may compromise its specialized capabilities in handling complex problems. |
| Qwen3 kept the long-CoT cold-start SFT deliberately small, minimizing both samples and steps, so as not to limit the gains of the following RL stage. | 2505.09388 — Qwen3 Technical Report | 10 | The objective at this stage is to instill foundational reasoning patterns in the model without overly emphasizing immediate reasoning performance. This approach ensures that the model's potential is not limited, allowing for greater flexibility and improvement during the subsequent reinforcement learning (RL) phase. To achieve this objective effectively, it is preferable to minimize both the number of training samples and the training steps during this preparatory phase. |

| Tina ran GRPO training and vLLM generation on the same two GPUs by capping vLLM's memory, trading wall-clock time for a smaller hardware footprint. | 2504.15777 — Tina: Tiny Reasoning Models via LoRA | 5 | To enable this, we co-located the RL training process and the vLLM on the same two GPUs by constraining vLLM's GPU memory usage. The training itself utilized data parallelism across both GPUs. While running inference and training concurrently on two GPUs might result in a longer wall-clock training time compared to a setup with dedicated inference GPUs, it significantly reduces the hardware requirement. |

| The math-expert Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B showed a much smaller long-CoT gap than the general Qwen2.5-1.5B and gained more from large-teacher CoT, and the authors conjecture that the gap stems mainly from the student's limited in-domain knowledge. | 2502.12143 — Small Models Struggle to Learn from Strong Reasoners | 5 | Our findings show that the long CoT gap of the small math expert model is significantly smaller than that of general small models. We conjecture that a key factor leading to the small model learnability gap is the limited in-domain knowledge of small student models. |
