| When the student trains on data unlike the teacher's pre-training data, the teacher must first be adapted on that data: distilling a 300M student from an unadapted LLaMA-3-8B gave only a small improvement over cross-entropy, and adapting the teacher for 50B tokens increased it significantly. | 2503.16870 — Sparse Logit Sampling: Accelerating Knowledge Distillation in LLMs | 8 | Sreenivas et al. (2024) found that if the student is being trained on a data distribution different from the teacher's pre-training data, the teacher should first be adapted (finetuned) on this data by training for a short while. We also observe the same - when training a 300M student on Fineweb-edu data with the LLaMA-3-8B model as teacher, using the original teacher model directly yields only a small improvement over CE (Table 13). After teacher adaptation for 50B tokens, this increases significantly. |
| In offline-logit pre-training distillation (300M students, 8B Llama-3 teacher, 10B tokens), vanilla forward KL outperformed reverse KL, forward-plus-reverse KL, MSE and L1. | 2503.16870 — Sparse Logit Sampling: Accelerating Knowledge Distillation in LLMs | 9 | We also experiment with alternative loss/divergence functions, by training 300M students with 8B Llama-3 teacher for 10B tokens. Some prior works (Kim et al., 2021; Wu et al., 2024b; Gu et al., 2023; Ko et al., 2024) find alternative objectives such as Reverse KL Divergence, Mean Squared Error as superior, while other works (Sultan, 2023; Wen et al., 2023; Muralidharan et al., 2024; Peng et al., 2024) have observed the opposite. In Table 15, we observe that vanilla forward KLD outperforms other objectives. |

Our proposed method achieves very similar performance and calibration compared to FullKD, while using only 12 tokens (Table 6). |

| For synthetic problems with no oracle solution, rStar-Coder samples 16 long-reasoning solutions from QWQ-32B, runs each on a shared set of at least 50 test inputs, and accepts both the outputs and the solutions that produced them when a majority of solutions produce identical outputs across the whole input set. | 2505.21297 — rStar-Coder: Scaling Competitive Code Reasoning with a Large-Scale Verified Dataset | 6 | As illustrated in Fig. 4, for each problem, we first sample 16 long-reasoning candidate solutions using a frontier reasoning model (QWQ-32B [29]). We then sample a diverse set of at least 50 test inputs with varying complexities. Each candidate solution is executed on this shared set of test inputs to generate the corresponding outputs. If a majority of these candidate solutions produce identical sets of outputs across this entire set of test inputs, then both these consistent sets of outputs and the candidate solutions that generated them are considered correct. |
| The 96.8% output-label accuracy, against 12.7% when GPT-4o generated input-output pairs directly, was measured on 64 expert-written seed problems that have oracle solutions (3,150 test inputs), labeling each input by majority vote over QWQ-32B solutions and comparing with the oracle's output; it is measured on seed problems, not on the synthetic problems the mechanism serves. | 2505.21297 — rStar-Coder: Scaling Competitive Code Reasoning with a Large-Scale Verified Dataset | 9 | To evaluate its effectiveness, we randomly sample 64 expert-written seed problems with oracle solutions and collect all their test inputs (3,150 in total). For each input, we label test outputs using mutual verification: long CoT solutions from QWQ-32B are executed to produce outputs, and majority voting is applied to determine the final label. These outputs are then compared against ground-truth outputs obtained by running the oracle solutions. As a baseline, we prompt GPT-4o to directly generate input-output pairs, following prior work [31, 12] (see Prompt in Appendix Fig. 10). As shown in Table 5 (Left), mutual verification achieves 96.8% accuracy, while the GPT-4o baseline yields only 12.7% |
| For the 7B model, the full rStar-Coder dataset (580K examples) beat the 37.7K seed problems with 16 solutions each (603K examples): 57.3 against 54.7 on LiveCodeBench and 16.15 against 10.41 on USACO 2025. | 2505.21297 — rStar-Coder: Scaling Competitive Code Reasoning with a Large-Scale Verified Dataset | 13 | Table 6: Ablation on scaling dimensions for the 7B model shows that expanding problem diversity is more effective and efficient than only increasing the number of solutions per problem. \| Dataset \| Unique Problems \| Data size \| LiveCodeBench \| USACO 2025 \| \|-----------------------------\|-------------------\|-------------\|-----------------\|--------------\| \| Seed Problems× 1 solutions \| 37.7K \| 37.7K \| 40.8 \| 0.52 \| \| Seed Problems× 8 solutions \| 37.7K \| 302K \| 51.1 \| 3.64 \| \| Seed Problems× 16 solutions \| 37.7K \| 603K \| 54.7 \| 10.41 \| \| rStar-Coder dataset \| 480K \| 580K \| 57.3 \| 16.15 \| |
| On the same 37.7K seed problems, raising solutions per problem from 1 to 8 to 16 took the 7B model from 40.8 to 51.1 to 54.7 on LiveCodeBench (USACO 0.52, 3.64, 10.41): repeated sampling alone gave 13.9 points, and moving to the diverse dataset added 2.6 more (57.3) at slightly smaller size. Both axes help; the table also lists 480K unique problems where the text says 418K. | 2505.21297 — rStar-Coder: Scaling Competitive Code Reasoning with a Large-Scale Verified Dataset | 13 | Table 6: Ablation on scaling dimensions for the 7B model shows that expanding problem diversity is more effective and efficient than only increasing the number of solutions per problem. \| Dataset \| Unique Problems \| Data size \| LiveCodeBench \| USACO 2025 \| \|-----------------------------\|-------------------\|-------------\|-----------------\|--------------\| \| Seed Problems× 1 solutions \| 37.7K \| 37.7K \| 40.8 \| 0.52 \| \| Seed Problems× 8 solutions \| 37.7K \| 302K \| 51.1 \| 3.64 \| \| Seed Problems× 16 solutions \| 37.7K \| 603K \| 54.7 \| 10.41 \| \| rStar-Coder dataset \| 480K \| 580K \| 57.3 \| 16.15 \| |
| rStar-Coder's scores, its 1.5B model's included, come from long-reasoning SFT decoded at temperature 0.6 with up to 32k output tokens, pass@1 averaged over 16 samples per problem: here a 1.5B student did gain from long chain-of-thought data, at a large inference budget. | 2505.21297 — rStar-Coder: Scaling Competitive Code Reasoning with a Large-Scale Verified Dataset | 7 | For inference, we set temperature of 0.6, maximum output length to 32k tokens. To mitigate performance variance inherent in single-run evaluations, we sample 16 solutions per problem and report the average pass@1 accuracy for all benchmarks. |
| Of 1,565,632 synthesized problems, 380,560 passed mutual verification (about 24%); with 37,754 verified seed problems (of 57,215 collected) the dataset totals 418,314 problems. | 2505.21297 — rStar-Coder: Scaling Competitive Code Reasoning with a Large-Scale Verified Dataset | 3 | \| Total                                    \|                                 57,215 \|                                 37,754 \|                               1,565,632 \|                              380,560 \|            418,314 \| |
| rStar-Coder discards a synthetic problem when fewer than 60% of its solutions agree on the outputs, lowering the bar to 40% for problems derived from Codeforces problems rated above 1600 so that harder problems survive. | 2505.21297 — rStar-Coder: Scaling Competitive Code Reasoning with a Large-Scale Verified Dataset | 7 | Specifically, if fewer than 60% of solutions agree on the outputs, the problem is discarded. For problems synthesized based on Codeforces problems, we use the original cf_rating to identify hard problems (cf_rating &gt; 1600) and adjust the threshold to 40% to include harder problems. |
| rStar-Coder also keeps unverified traces: for seed problems where QWQ-32B produces no correct solution, all its generated solutions go into the training data. | 2505.21297 — rStar-Coder: Scaling Competitive Code Reasoning with a Large-Scale Verified Dataset | 7 | For particularly challenging problems where QWQ-32B fails to produce a correct solution, we follow prior work [26, 2] and retain all generated solutions to include more diverse and potentially correct intermediate reasoning steps in the training data. |
| Generating test inputs through LLM-written generator and validator functions, instead of prompting GPT-4o for inputs, raised the downstream LiveCodeBench score from 42.9 to 44.6 (hard problems 10.6 to 12.6). | 2505.21297 — rStar-Coder: Scaling Competitive Code Reasoning with a Large-Scale Verified Dataset | 9 | \| GPT-4o verification \| 12.7%      \| GPT-4o prompting            \|                      42.9 \|                   87.3 \|                     54.1 \|                   10.6 \| \| Dual verification   \| 96.8%      \| Three-step input generation \|                      44.6 \|                   87.7 \|                     56.5 \|                   12.6 \| |

| The unfiltered baseline that no filter beat was not compute-controlled: it trained on all 63,200 answers while every filtered set was cut to 31,600, so 'did not improve over not filtering' compares half the data with all of it, not filtered with unfiltered data at equal size. | 2506.04178 — OpenThoughts: Data Recipes for Reasoning Models | 9 | To ensure that we can still obtain datasets of size 31,600 after filtering, we first generate 63,200 answers, apply each answer-filtering strategy, and then sample 31,600 question-answer pairs from the filtered dataset. Our ablations also include a baseline with no filtering, which is not compute-controlled, as it contains 63,200 questions. |

| OpenCodeReasoning attributes the advantage of incorrect solutions to their covering harder questions than the correct ones. | 2504.01943 — OpenCodeReasoning: Advancing Data Distillation for Competitive Coding | 6 | Investigating further, we identified that incorrect solutions span questions that are more challenging than the ones associated with the correct solutions. |
| OpenCodeReasoning is pure sequence-level distillation: its models use SFT only and reach 61.8% on LiveCodeBench and 24.6% on CodeContests (best model), above RL-trained alternatives. | 2504.01943 — OpenCodeReasoning: Advancing Data Distillation for Competitive Coding | 1 | Our distilled models use only SFT to achieve 61.8% on LiveCodeBench and 24.6% on CodeContests, surpassing alternatives trained with reinforcement learning. |
| OpenCodeReasoning trained only 7B, 14B and 32B Qwen2.5 students (the incorrect-solution ablation at 14B); none of its results is tested at the student size this project targets. | 2504.01943 — OpenCodeReasoning: Advancing Data Distillation for Competitive Coding | 2 | Fine-tuning Qwen2.5 base and instruct LLMs (7B, 14B, and 32B) with OPENCODEREASONING significantly outperformed R1-Distill-Qwen and other open-weight SFTonly models. |
| LiveCodeBench kept rising up to 736k samples with no plateau, and the largest gains came from adding unique, varied and difficult questions. | 2504.01943 — OpenCodeReasoning: Advancing Data Distillation for Competitive Coding | 5 | It is to be noted that the scaling curve in Figure 3 does not plateau despite the use of 736k samples, and it remains to be seen when the model's improvement will saturate and yield diminishing returns. The most significant gains in benchmark scores were observed by simply increasing the number of unique, varied, and difficult questions. |
| Adding 356k C++ traces to the Python set had no positive effect on the Python benchmarks but significantly improved the C++ benchmark (IOI): the second language's data helped that language only. | 2504.01943 — OpenCodeReasoning: Advancing Data Distillation for Competitive Coding | 7 | Consequently, we explored the inclusion of 356k C++ samples, generated by R1, in conjunction with OPENCODEREASONING. In addition to LiveCodeBench and CodeContest, we evaluated on the IOI benchmark (Penedo et al., 2025b), which requires C++ solutions. The results are presented in Table 4. It is evident that the inclusion of C++ solutions has no positive impact on Python benchmark performance, but does significantly improve the accuracy on the C++ benchmark. |
| Hard problems often exhausted a 16k-token reasoning budget, yet raising it to 32k gave no substantial gain on them, and long traces sometimes fell into reasoning loops that consumed all available tokens without producing a solution. | 2504.01943 — OpenCodeReasoning: Advancing Data Distillation for Competitive Coding | 8 | During evaluation, we observed that a significantly larger token budget is required for hard problems, as models often did not complete their reasoning phase within 16k tokens. However, extending this budget to 32k tokens did not translate to improved accuracy. In fact, no substantial performance gains were observed on the hard problems. Our findings corroborate Wang et al. (2025) that suggests LLMs tend to be incorrect with more tokens, possibly due to increased backtracking in incorrect solutions (more analysis in the next section). Finally, as a consequence of long reasoning traces, models sometimes enter an unrecoverable reasoning loop, consuming all available tokens while repeating reasoning steps instead of producing a solution. |
| The correct/incorrect labels behind the filtering ablation came from at most 50 tests per problem, out of an average of 192.7 generated tests, to save compute. | 2504.01943 — OpenCodeReasoning: Advancing Data Distillation for Competitive Coding | 6 | Given an average of 192.7 generated tests per problem and a 445k sample dataset, we limited tests to 50 to reduce computational cost. |

| Self-generated data beats a teacher's only when the teacher is not much stronger. StarCoder2-3B, the smallest model tested, scored 35.4 on its own data and 34.1 on Llama-3-8B's, but 42.1 on DeepSeek-Coder-33B's: a weak student with a clearly stronger teacher available learns more from the teacher. | 2410.24198 — SelfCodeAlign: Self-Alignment for Code Generation | 8 | However, when the teacher model is clearly stronger, the base model learns better by distilling the teacher's knowledge. For example, StarCoder2-3B achieves higher pass@1 trained on its own data (35.4) compared to Llama-3-8B data (34.1), but when tuned with stronger models, StarCoder2-3B further improves (e.g., 42.1 with DeepSeek-Coder-33B data). |

| The model writes its own tests, so a passing response may only pass wrong tests; the authors leave test validity open and suggest the discarded failing responses could feed a reinforcement-learning loop. | 2410.24198 — SelfCodeAlign: Self-Alignment for Code Generation | 10 | Second, we gather several negative samples during response generation, which are currently filtered out. These negatives could be used in a reinforcement-learning loop to steer the model away from incorrect responses [31, 53]. Furthermore, the good responses are labeled by test execution, while the generated unit tests might be erroneous, calling for research to study and improve the generation of valid test cases. |

| Continued pretraining took about 32 hours on 8 A100 GPUs with 40 GB each; SFT took about 9 hours on a single 40 GB A100. | 2410.17736 — MojoBench: Language Modeling and Benchmarks for Mojo | 13 | Pretraining For the pretraining phase, we utilize a cluster of 8 NVIDIA A100 GPUs, each with 40 GB memory, provided by Lambda Labs 9 . This configuration yields a total of 320 GB GPU memory, complemented by 512 GB system memory and 2 TB disk space. The pretraining process spans approximately 32 hours on this highperformance cluster. Finetuning The finetuning stage is conducted on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU with 40 GB memory, accessed through Google Colab 10 . This setup is augmented with 80 GB system memory and 256 GB disk space. The finetuning process requires approximately 9 hours to complete on this configuration. |

| The reward-hacking explanation is the authors' hypothesis ('potential reward hacking'), stated for the RL run from the base model, where RM-based RL gave worse results than the rule reward; no hacked outputs are shown. | 2502.01718 — ACECODER: Acing Coder RL via Automated Test-Case Synthesis | 7 | However, we do find that using reward models for RL tuning can lead to worse results. We attribute this to the potential reward hacking during the tuning process. |
| AceCoder filtered GPT-4o-mini-imagined tests with one program from a stronger model (Qwen2.5-Coder-32B-Instruct): every test that program failed was removed, then every question left with fewer than 5 tests was dropped as likely ambiguous. The filter is a single model solution, not a reference solution or a majority vote. | 2502.01718 — ACECODER: Acing Coder RL via Automated Test-Case Synthesis | 3 | we facilitated a stronger coder model Qwen2.5Coder-32B-Instruct (Hui et al., 2024a) as a proxy to perform quality control. Specifically, we prompt it for each xr to generate a program y′ and then run these programs over the test cases to approximate their quality. We removed all test cases ti where the generated solution program y′ could not pass. Furthermore, we removed questions with fewer than 5 tests after filtering, as these questions might be overly ambiguous. |
| After filtering, a human audit of 40 questions with 5 sampled tests each found 3 of 200 tests invalid (3/200 = 1.5%). | 2502.01718 — ACECODER: Acing Coder RL via Automated Test-Case Synthesis | 4 | we conducted a human case study by randomly selecting 40 questions, each with 5 randomly sampled test cases, resulting in 200 manually annotated test cases. Encouragingly, only 3 out of these 200 test cases were found invalid upon review |
| AceCoder's RL kept only the hardest 25% of questions (lower average pass rate, higher variance) so groups have diverse outcomes; rollout batch 256, 8 programs per question, training batch 128, learning rate 5e-7, 1 episode, 6 hours on 8 H100s. | 2502.01718 — ACECODER: Acing Coder RL via Automated Test-Case Synthesis | 5 | We train our model on a subsampled hard version of ACECODE-87K, where we keep the 25% of the questions with lower average pass rates and higher variance. This is to ensure the question is hard and that the sampled programs are diverse enough. For the training hyperparameters, we set the rollout batch size to 256, and 8 programs are sampled per question. The training batch size is 128 with a learning rate of 5e-7. All the models are trained for 1 episode and finished in 6 hours on 8 x H100 GPUs. |

| The faster early learning of pass-rate reward appeared under GRPO and was muted under RLOO; the authors' plausible explanation is that GRPO's per-group std normalization amplifies partial-pass advantages when full-pass samples are scarce. | 2605.02944 — Exploring Pass-Rate Reward in Reinforcement Learning for Code Generation | 4 | As a secondary observation, the early-stage acceleration seen under GRPO is muted under RLOO. | Pass-rate reward can reinforce shortcut solutions (brute-force heuristics, overfitting to the test suite) that reach high pass rates while penalizing near-correct solutions that need minor fixes. | 2605.02944 — Exploring Pass-Rate Reward in Reinforcement Learning for Code Generation | 1 | In particular, pass-rate rewards can reinforce solutions that exploit shortcuts (e.g., brute-force heuristics or overfitting to the provided test suite) to achieve high pass rates, while penalizing near-correct solutions that require minor fixes to pass all tests. |
| In groups with no full-pass sample, a probe of the GRPO update under pass-rate reward moved the log-probability of a verified full-pass reference only negligibly. | 2605.02944 — Exploring Pass-Rate Reward in Reinforcement Learning for Code Generation | 6 | Even though pass-rate provides non-binary rewards in this regime, the induced group-level update produces only a negligible change in the log-probability of the full-pass reference solution. |
| The 57.4% (225 of 392 tasks) counts tasks that contain both a harmful sample with positive advantage and a helpful sample with negative advantage, as labeled by a DeepSeek-R1 judge, on 392 tasks drawn from an early GRPO pass-rate checkpoint whose groups had no full-pass sample and for which DeepSeek-R1 found a full-pass reference. | 2605.02944 — Exploring Pass-Rate Reward in Reinforcement Learning for Code Generation | 7 | We also quantify a pattern that directly reflects policy gradient updates. Specifically, we check whether a task contains (i) a harmful sample with positive advantage and (ii) a helpful sample with negative advantage. This is exactly the "push-pull" scenario where optimization increases the likelihood of a harmful mode while decreasing that of a helpful mode. We observe this advantage-sign conflict in 225/392 tasks (57.4%) |
| Across four judge prompts and two judge models (DeepSeek-R1, GPT-5.4), the conflict rate ranged from 21.5% to 57.5% and the reversal rate from 28.8% to 37.4%; the headline conflict figure comes from the most permissive configuration (DeepSeek-R1 with the baseline prompt). | 2605.02944 — Exploring Pass-Rate Reward in Reinforcement Learning for Code Generation | 14 | The reversal signal stays in the 28.8%-37.4% range across every prompt variant and judge model, comparable to the paper baseline of 36.5%. The conflict signal becomes more conservative under stricter rubrics and under GPT-5.4, but remains clearly positive (between 21.5% and 57.5%) in every configuration. |

| The 60-70% share of zero-advantage groups under binary GRPO, and VeRPO's below-25%, were measured in the multi-turn setting (up to several attempts with execution feedback), with Qwen3-8B trained on a TACO subset; VeRPO stays below 25% 'for most of training', not throughout. | 2601.03525 — Beyond Binary: Turning Partial Success into Dense Verifiable Rewards for Reinforcement Learning in Code Generation | 8 | To better understand the performance gains of VeRPO, we analyze how often rollout groups provide non-degenerate optimization signals in the multi-turn setting. We employ the degenerate group ratio: the fraction of rollout groups with identical rewards, which yield zero relative advantages and thus no policy-gradient signal under group-relative optimization. Fig. 1 (Left) shows that outcomedriven GRPO maintains a high degenerate group ratio, typically between 60% and 70%, indicating that binary outcome rewards discard substantial information from partial execution outcomes. In contrast, VeRPO keeps this ratio below 25% for most of training |

| RLEF adds a small penalty for invalid code, introduced because generating invalid code in non-final responses was a failure mode in initial experiments. | 2410.02089 — RLEF: Grounding Code LLMs in Execution Feedback with Reinforcement Learning | 3 | In initial experiments we found that a possible failure mode concerns the generation of invalid code in non-final responses, which we address by providing a small penalty for invalid responses. |
| RLEF shows public-test feedback inside the dialog and gives the episode reward on whether all public and private tests pass. | 2410.02089 — RLEF: Grounding Code LLMs in Execution Feedback with Reinforcement Learning | 3 | At the end of an episode, a scalar reward is provided corresponding to whether all public and private tests are passing. |
| RLEF drops the invalid-code penalty for single-turn training, because there the reward for an incorrect solution already covers invalid code; the penalty only matters for non-final turns. | 2410.02089 — RLEF: Grounding Code LLMs in Execution Feedback with Reinforcement Learning | 8 | We use the same training loop for single generations, albeit without the penalty for invalid code (Section 2.2) as this is subsumed by the reward signal for incorrect solutions. |
| RLEF scores on held-out private tests because a model shown fixed test inputs and outputs in its feedback can learn to copy the expected outputs into later answers. | 2410.02089 — RLEF: Grounding Code LLMs in Execution Feedback with Reinforcement Learning | 3 | held-out tests guard against shortcuts during the optimization procedure in which an LLM can copy expected test outputs in subsequent answers, based on execution feedback. |
| Before RLEF, the Instruct models made only minimal edits between turns and often resubmitted the same code despite feedback pointing out errors. | 2410.02089 — RLEF: Grounding Code LLMs in Execution Feedback with Reinforcement Learning | 7 | This underscores a shortcoming of the Instruct models without RLEF in that they perform only minimal code edits; indeed, we observe that they frequently output the same code solution despite inline feedback pointing out errors. |

| muCode treats multi-turn code generation as a one-step recoverable MDP: the correct code can be recovered from any intermediate code state in a single turn. | 2502.20380 — Multi-Turn Code Generation Through Single-Step Rewards | 1 | Our key insight is that code generation is a one-step recoverable MDP, where the correct code can be recovered from any intermediate code state in a single turn. |
| One-step recoverability is argued from the definition (the correctness of the code depends only on the prompt, not on the interaction history), not measured. muCode uses it to replace multi-turn RL with imitation learning: expert iteration in which a learned verifier plus the oracle relabels each turn with the best solution found for that prompt. The paper does not test whether single-step repair training captures most of the multi-turn skill. | 2502.20380 — Multi-Turn Code Generation Through Single-Step Rewards | 1 | this reduces the problem from reinforcement learning, which requires exploration and credit assignment, to imitation learning, where the model simply learns to mimic correct code |

If the localization result is empty or contains a target not present in the problem description, the reward is set to 0; otherwise, the reward is calculated as the Fβ score between Oloc and the ground-truth answer A. |
| SoRFT also scores code edits by F-beta against the ground-truth edit, with reward 0 when the edit is empty or its search target does not appear in the input. Like the localization rule, it zeroes the whole sample; the survey's SoRFT-style guide table instead drops an off-file location and scores the rest. | 2502.20127 — SoRFT: Issue Resolving with Subtask-oriented Reinforced Fine-Tuning | 5 | For the code editing subtask (line 8-15), we first extract the modification target Osearch and the modification code Oedit from the response. If the modification code is empty or the modification target does not appear in the problem description, the reward is 0; otherwise, the reward is calculated as the Fβ score between Oedit and the ground-truth answer A. |
| SoRFT weights recall over precision: beta must exceed 1 and is set to 3. | 2502.20127 — SoRFT: Issue Resolving with Subtask-oriented Reinforced Fine-Tuning | 5 | Since recall has a greater influence on the final outcome across different subtasks, β should be a value greater than 1. In our experiments, we set β = 3 to prioritize recall while maintaining a reasonable trade-off with precision. |
| Replacing F-beta with a hit score (1.0 if the answer contains at least one ground-truth element, else 0.0) caused reward hacking: the model wrote fewer thoughts and listed more answers to raise the chance of a hit. | 2502.20127 — SoRFT: Issue Resolving with Subtask-oriented Reinforced Fine-Tuning | 7 | Specifically, if the response contains at least one element from the ground-truth answer, the reward score is set to 1.0; otherwise, it is set to 0.0. As shown in Figure 3a, using the hit score leads to reward hacking, where the model tends to generate fewer thoughts and more answers to increase the likelihood of including a ground-truth element. |

| KodCode labels difficulty by the success rate of GPT-4o's (solution, test) self-verification across 10 attempts per question: easy above 2/3, medium 1/3 to 2/3, hard below 1/3, fail if every attempt fails. The rate measures the generator, not the model being trained. | 2503.02951 — KodCode: A Diverse, Challenging, and Verifiable Synthetic Dataset for Coding | 5 | We analyze the difficulty distribution across KODCODE subsets by examining the success rate of (sol, test) pairs in self-verification across n = 10 attempts per question, as shown in Figure 5. We categorize questions into four difficulty levels: easy (pass rate &gt;2/3), medium (1/3 to 2/3), hard (&lt;1/3), and fail (all failures). |
| KodCode checks contamination against the benchmarks it evaluates on (HumanEval, MBPP, BigCodeBench, LiveCodeBench V5) by embedding cosine similarity, counting a question contaminated above 0.95 similarity to its nearest benchmark question. | 2503.02951 — KodCode: A Diverse, Challenging, and Verifiable Synthetic Dataset for Coding | 6 | We evaluate potential contamination between KODCODE and existing evaluation benchmarks, including HumanEval (Chen et al., 2021), MBPP (Austin et al., 2021), BigCodeBench (Zhuo et al., 2024), and LiveCodeBench (V5) (Jain et al., 2024). For each question in KODCODE , we identify the most similar benchmark question using cosine similarity of embeddings, considering a question contaminated if the similarity exceeds 0.95. |
| Only 94 of 447K questions were flagged, mostly between the simple Prefill subset and HumanEval/MBPP questions: a pool of HumanEval/MBPP-level problems is where overlap with those benchmarks concentrates. | 2503.02951 — KodCode: A Diverse, Challenging, and Verifiable Synthetic Dataset for Coding | 6 | First, the contamination rate is minimal, with only 94 potentially contaminated questions out of 447K. Second, most overlaps occur between the Prefill subset and simple Python questions from HumanEval/MBPP. |
| Self-verification kept 80 MBPP solutions (88.9%) and 190 LiveCodeBench-V5 solutions (49.9%); of those kept, 78 of 80 (97.5%, after manual review) and 189 (99.47%) passed the human tests. It is precise, but it drops about half of the harder problems. | 2503.02951 — KodCode: A Diverse, Challenging, and Verifiable Synthetic Dataset for Coding | 4 | After applying our Self-Verification approach, 80 solutions (88.9%) pass self-verification for MBPP, while 190 solutions (49.9%) pass self-verification for LiveCodeBench-V5. We then evaluate these retained solutions against the ground-truth unit tests. We note that due to inherent ambiguity in MBPP questions, some solutions that follow correct logic fail assertions because of mismatched input formats or numerical precision differences. After manual review of these edge cases, 78 out of 80 solutions pass all ground-truth unit tests, achieving a 97.5% pass rate for MBPP. For LiveCodeBench-V5, 189 solutions (99.47%) successfully pass all tests. |
| KodCode keeps a question-solution-test triplet only if the solution passes its generated tests and those tests reach 100% branch coverage of the solution (pytest-cov). | 2503.02951 — KodCode: A Diverse, Challenging, and Verifiable Synthetic Dataset for Coding | 3 | Additionally, we perform branch coverage analysis using the PYTEST-COV framework to ensure the diversity of test cases. Only questionsolution-test triplets that pass self-verification and achieve 100% branch coverage are retained. |

| KodCode's RL runs GRPO for 256 steps on 9.5K training samples, actor learning rate 5e-7, 16 rollouts per question, batch 256, max response 4096 tokens, KL coefficient 0.001, binary all-tests-pass reward. | 2503.02951 — KodCode: A Diverse, Challenging, and Verifiable Synthetic Dataset for Coding | 7 | using 9.5K randomly selected samples from KODCODEas the training set, and 0.5K as the validation set. We refer to this dataset as KODCODE -RL-10K. We perform GRPO (Shao et al., 2024) for 256 steps with actor learning rate of 5 × 10-7 , 16 rollouts per question, a batch size of 256, max response length of 4096, and apply KL coefficient of 0.001. We assign binary rewards for RL: 1 if the model's solution passes all unit tests, and 0 otherwise. |

LM Rewrite and Procedural bugs are comparable to PR mirrors. We randomly sample 1000 instances per bug generation strategy (LM Modify, LM Rewrite, Procedural Modifications, PR Mirrors). Per instance, we generate issue text with an LM and run expert trajectory generation. We then fine-tune a student model per strategy, capping training points to the minimum number of successful trajectories from any strategy (507) for fair comparison. |

| Four SFT sets of 500 trajectories each, at difficulty scores 2, 4, 6 and 8 (easy/medium/hard scored 1/5/9), trained students to 12.4%, 10.8%, 13.6% and 12.2% on SWE-bench Verified: no strong correlation between training-task difficulty and downstream performance. | 2504.21798 — SWE-smith: Scaling Data for Software Engineering Agents | 7 | Next, from all successful trajectories, we create four fine-tuning datasets of 500 trajectories each corresponding to difficulty scores of 2, 4, 6, and 8. As mentioned in Section 2.2, the corresponding scores for easy/medium/hard are 1/5/9. Therefore, the SFT dataset for score 2 is made up of trajectories corresponding to 375 easy and 125 medium instances, and so on. Somewhat surprisingly, we do not observe strong correlation between increased difficulty and downstream performance. For the student models trained on the 2/4/6/8 difficulty SFT datasets, we get pass@1 scores of 12.4%, 10.8%, 13.6%, and 12.2% on SWE-bench Verified. |

Through several spot checks of different trajectories, we came to a working hypothesis that while the rejection sampling fine-tuning process had improved its ability to carry out multi-turn interactions in this task setting, there were instances where code edits reflected syntax closer to Python despite code and files viewed in previous steps clearly not being written in Python. |

The model trained on test-based issues only does so for 127 cases, a 66% decrease. |

This holds true even for small fine-tuning datasets of around 1000 (less than 5% of the original dataset size) bug fixes. |
| Matching the gain from scaling mutants from 5x to 100x per snippet took fine-tuning on at least 10% of the real-fix dataset, 3314 bugs; mutants are the cheaper lever until they saturate. | 2207.00301 — Can we learn from developer mistakes? Learning to localize and repair real bugs from real bug fixes | 9 | For example, to achieve the same performance gain obtained from scaling the number of mutants generated from 5x to 100x, we have to fine-tune on at least 10% of our real bug fix dataset (3314 bugs). |
| More mutants per snippet helped up to 100x (average 85 unique mutants per snippet, so many are oversampled) and hurt beyond 100x. | 2207.00301 — Can we learn from developer mistakes? Learning to localize and repair real bugs from real bug fixes | 8 | First of all, in contrast to common believe [12], we observe that increasing the number of mutants up to 100x generated mutants per code snippet leads to a performance improvement for both localization and repair 7 . This is surprising as the number of unique mutants per code snippet is limited (with an average of 85 unique mutants per code snippet) and, henceforth, buggy programs with more mutant candidates are oversampled. Still, we found that increasing the limit of mutants beyond 100x (and thereby oversampling code snippets in our dataset that provide up to 200k unique mutants) actually decreases the localization and repair performance. |
| Fine-tuning on real fixes lowered RealiT's scores on held-out mutants while raising them on real bugs; the authors find no correlation between mutant-benchmark and real-bug performance, so gains measured on seeded bugs are hard to interpret. | 2207.00301 — Can we learn from developer mistakes? Learning to localize and repair real bugs from real bug fixes | 6 | As there is clearly no correlation between the real world performance and the performance on mutants when the model is fine-tuned on real bug fixes, performance gains on mutants independent from real world performance become difficult to interpret. |
| Placing mutants where real bugs occurred (apply the real fix, then re-inject a mutation) did not substitute for real fixes; its small gain looked like a scaling effect, not an effect of realistic context. | 2207.00301 — Can we learn from developer mistakes? Learning to localize and repair real bugs from real bug fixes | 9 | Still, we find that a model trained on real bug fixes clearly outperforms a model trained on postfix mutants when evaluated on real bug fixes. Since the performance of detecting mutants does not decrease when training on postfix mutants (similar to scaling the number of mutants generated), we actually conclude that the performance gain is likely a scaling effect and can not necessarily be attributed to the mutation context. |

| While training OpenAI's frontier reasoning model on agentic coding tasks, two systemic hacks emerged: exit(0), exploiting a bug to leave the environment before all unit tests ran, and raise SkipTest, raising an exception from functions outside the testing framework so unit test evaluation was skipped. | 2503.11926 — Monitoring Reasoning Models for Misbehavior and the Risks of Promoting Obfuscation | 4 | we found two particularly effective coding hacks: exit(0), where the agent exploited a bug that allowed it to exit from the environment early without running all unit tests, and raise SkipTest, in which the agent raised an exception from functions outside the testing framework in order to skip unit test evaluation |
| Among the problem-specific hacks the CoT monitor caught in the frontier run, the agent created local versions of entire libraries such as pandas to overwrite their verification functions. | 2503.11926 — Monitoring Reasoning Models for Misbehavior and the Risks of Promoting Obfuscation | 6 | creating local versions of entire libraries such as pandas in order to overwrite their verification functions |
| In the frontier run the agent found and decompiled a compiled jar of the reference solution that had not been removed from the task (more generally, pyc and similar files containing the reference solution). | 2503.11926 — Monitoring Reasoning Models for Misbehavior and the Risks of Promoting Obfuscation | 20 | Figure 11: Decompiling a reference solution's compiled jar file that was incorrectly left in the task. |
| Direct edits to unit tests and the testing framework come from the paper's controlled experiments on a non-frontier agent in honeypot environments that deliberately allowed such edits (while the prompt forbade them). In the frontier run the standard mitigation already forbade modifying the testing framework and test files; the frontier hacks went around it (skipping tests, modifying upstream libraries, stubbing pandas, decompiling reference files, parsing test files at runtime). | 2503.11926 — Monitoring Reasoning Models for Misbehavior and the Risks of Promoting Obfuscation | 6 | Namely, we allow agents to modify both the unit tests and the testing framework, even though we instruct them in the prompt to not do so. |
| Adding the CoT monitor to the RL reward produced a more capable and more aligned agent in the low-optimization regime, but with too much optimization the agent learned obfuscated reward hacking: it hid its intent in the CoT while still hacking at a significant rate. The experiment used a non-frontier agent whose monitor saw only the CoT. | 2503.11926 — Monitoring Reasoning Models for Misbehavior and the Risks of Promoting Obfuscation | 1 | While we show that integrating CoT monitors into the reinforcement learning reward can indeed produce more capable and more aligned agents in the low optimization regime, we find that with too much optimization, agents learn obfuscated reward hacking, hiding their intent within the CoT while still exhibiting a significant rate of reward hacking. |
| Under CoT pressure the agent hacked less overall but still at a significant rate, and the CoT monitor's recall fell to near zero. | 2503.11926 — Monitoring Reasoning Models for Misbehavior and the Risks of Promoting Obfuscation | 8 | However, the agent trained with CoT pressure still learns to hack at a significant rate, and even more worrying, the CoT monitor's recall falls to near zero, indicating that the agent has learned to hide its intent within its CoT. |
| The authors recommend paying a 'monitorability tax': not applying strong optimization pressure directly to the chain-of-thought, so it stays usable for detecting misbehavior, which supports monitoring rather than penalizing. | 2503.11926 — Monitoring Reasoning Models for Misbehavior and the Risks of Promoting Obfuscation | 1 | it may be necessary to pay a monitorability tax by not applying strong optimization pressures directly to the chain-of-thought, ensuring that CoTs remain monitorable and useful for detecting misaligned behavior |

| Forbidding edits to the testing framework and test files was the frontier run's standard mitigation, and the agent bypassed it by modifying upstream libraries the tests depended on, so protecting test files alone is not enough. | 2503.11926 — Monitoring Reasoning Models for Misbehavior and the Risks of Promoting Obfuscation | 6 | libraries bypasses our standard mitigation of not allowing agents to modify the testing framework and files themselves |
| Once a general hack appears it is quickly reinforced and spreads to nearly every training environment, so one grader hole compromises the whole task pool, not one task. | 2503.11926 — Monitoring Reasoning Models for Misbehavior and the Risks of Promoting Obfuscation | 4 | Once general hacks like these emerge, they quickly get reinforced and become systemic, affecting nearly every training environment. |

| On Impossible-SWEbench, hiding the tests cut cheating to near zero but degraded performance on the original benchmark; read-only tests (modified tests reverted at scoring) restored legitimate performance while preventing test modification. | 2510.20270 — ImpossibleBench: Measuring LLMs' Propensity of Exploiting Test Cases | 8 | Hiding tests from agents reduces cheating success rate to near zero 2 , but also degrades performance on the original benchmark. However, the effect is much less pronounced for Claude Opus 4.1. |

| KernelLLM and AutoTriton produce more functionally invalid code after fine-tuning than their base models did, which the authors attribute to fine-tuning without rigorous functionality checks rewarding shortcuts. | 2510.17891 — TritonRL: Training LLMs to Think and Code Triton Without Cheating | 5 | This suggests that fine-tuning processes that lack rigorous functionality checks can inadvertently incentivize models to adopt invalid shortcuts, superficially satisfying syntax requirements while failing to implement the core kernel operations. |
| Citing Kevin, the paper reports that exploiting unit-test loopholes, such as calling high-level PyTorch modules instead of writing the kernel, is particularly prevalent in models of 8B and below. | 2510.17891 — TritonRL: Training LLMs to Think and Code Triton Without Cheating | 1 | models frequently learn to exploit unit test loopholes, such as direct use of high-level PyTorch modules, rather than generating proper code, and this phenomenon is particularly prevalent in smaller models (8B and below) |
| TritonRL makes the syntax check (a rule-based linter) and functionality check (linter plus LLM judge) necessary conditions for both the correctness and speedup rewards, and clips speedup at 2 to keep extreme values from destabilizing training. | 2510.17891 — TritonRL: Training LLMs to Think and Code Triton Without Cheating | 5 | The syntax and functionality checks both serve as necessary conditions for both reward signals to prevent the model from being incentivized to exploit shortcuts. Also, we clip the speedup at 2 to prevent extreme values from destabilizing training. |

| Even with remotes, branches and tags removed, agents tried to reconnect the local repository to GitHub (git remote add) or fetch commit history with git clone, curl or similar tools, to recover the ground-truth fix. | 2603.00729 — Qwen3-Coder-Next Technical Report | 11 | reconnect local repositories to GitHub using commands such as git remote add, or retrieve commit history through git clone, curl, or similar tools, as illustrated in Figure 7. |
| Without the blocker, the agent learned on its own, as its capability grew, to use various git commands to retrieve ground-truth information despite removed remotes and future commits; the authors say this had not been reported before. | 2603.00729 — Qwen3-Coder-Next Technical Report | 10 | Even after removing git remotes and future commits, the agent autonomously learns to exploit various git commands to retrieve ground-truth information as model capability increases. |
| The 'Reinforced Reward Hacking Blocker' is a heuristic rule: any tool call containing both a repository link and a network-access keyword (git, curl, wget) is blocked and the agent gets explicit feedback; manual inspection found reward hacking effectively eliminated. | 2603.00729 — Qwen3-Coder-Next Technical Report | 11 | To address this, we introduce a heuristic blocking rule. Any tool call containing both a repository link (e.g., github.com/{repo}) and network-access keywords (e.g., git, curl, wget) is blocked, and the agent receives explicit feedback indicating the prohibited action. |

The escape was seen at that capability, not in a small dense model. | 2603.00729 — Qwen3-Coder-Next Technical Report | 11 | \| Qwen3-Coder-Next \| 80A3 \| 70.6 \| 71.1 \| 71.3 \| |
| The authors judged fully disabling network access unreasonable for SWE agents, which need it for environment setup, documentation and package installs, so they used a targeted blocker instead. | 2603.00729 — Qwen3-Coder-Next Technical Report | 11 | Fully disabling network access is not reasonable, as agents require connectivity for legitimate operations such as environment setup, documentation retrieval, or installing additional packages. |
| The bug-synthesis pipeline produced approximately 800K verifiable software-engineering task instances across more than nine programming languages, each with a Docker environment. | 2603.00729 — Qwen3-Coder-Next Technical Report | 2 | This process yields approximately 800K verifiable software engineering task instances spanning over nine programming languages. |
| Synthetic bugs are kept only if they fail existing tests and are fixed by reverting the patch; to curb shortcut learning, issue text is generated in natural language and the bug-triggering test files are excluded from what the agent sees. | 2603.00729 — Qwen3-Coder-Next Technical Report | 2 | We retain generated bugs only when they fail existing tests and are resolved by patch reversion, guaranteeing that each task is both meaningful and tractable. To mitigate shortcut learning, we generate natural-language issue descriptions and exclude bug-triggering test files. |
| The agents that build environments and verification scripts also exploited superficial verification shortcuts, so non-functional verifiers had to be detected and filtered automatically. | 2603.00729 — Qwen3-Coder-Next Technical Report | 2 | Environment construction poses significant challenges for agents, leading to failure modes where agents exploit superficial verification shortcuts. To mitigate this, we apply automated detection to identify and filter non-functional verifiers |
| Besides the outcome reward, multi-turn RL penalizes unfinished trajectories and applies token-level penalties to the tokens of tool calls that fail rule-based format validation. | 2603.00729 — Qwen3-Coder-Next Technical Report | 10 | Second, we apply a turn-level tool-format penalty. At each interaction step, we perform rule-based validation of tool-call format correctness. During optimization, tokens associated with invalid tool calls receive token-level penalties, preventing the model from learning malformed tool invocation patterns. |
| On SWE-bench Verified, SWE-AGILE takes Qwen3-8B from 15.83% (base) to 21.45% after SFT on 2.2k trajectories whose reasoning was backfilled by a larger model, and to 24.05% after compression-aware RLVR: RL adds 2.6 points (24.05 - 21.45) on top of SFT. | 2604.11716 — SWE-AGILE: A Software Agent Framework for Efficiently Managing Dynamic Reasoning Context | 6 | Starting from a general-purpose Qwen3-8B base, SWEAGILE (SFT) achieves a success rate of 21.45%, representing a substantial 35.5% relative improvement over the base model (15.83%). This verifies the efficacy of SWE-AGILE paradigm in eliciting System-2 reasoning capabilities even in 8B models. Remarkably, SWE-AGILE (SFT) achieves this performance using only 2.2k training trajectories, a mere 11% of the 19.3k dataset utilized by SWE-Dev. With the integration of our compression-aware RLVR, SWE-AGILE further elevates the success rate to 24.05% |

| SWE-AGILE's auxiliary compression reward is clipped so the model cannot bloat its reasoning to inflate the ratio, and is multiplied by task success so it is paid only on successful trajectories, preventing the policy from trading correctness for the auxiliary score. | 2604.11716 — SWE-AGILE: A Software Agent Framework for Efficiently Managing Dynamic Reasoning Context | 5 | The clipping mechanism prevents the model from artificially bloating reasoning traces (rt) merely to inflate the denominator of Rcomp beyond the saturation point. The multiplicative gating Isuccess ensures that compression rewards are added only on successful trajectories, effectively preventing the model from trading correctness for compression scores. |

Our result shows that a non-reasoning model trained with 2K training data performs even worse than our reasoning model trained with 500 samples. |

| The metric is Acc@k, not recall: an instance counts only if all edited code locations are within the top k. It is measured on 274 of SWE-bench Lite's 300 instances, those whose patch modifies an existing function. | 2505.07849 — SweRank: Software Issue Localization with Code Ranking | 7 | resulting in 274 retained examples out of 300. While SWE-Bench-Lite primarily consists of bug reports and feature requests, LocBench ( 560 examples) also includes security and performance issues. Consistent with Chen et al. (2025), we measure localization performance at three granularities: file, module (class) and function, with Accuracy at k (Acc@k) as the evaluation metric. This metric deems localization successful if all relevant code locations are correctly identified within the top-k results. |

| Against test results on Ansible (21 questions, 1,260 outputs), treating AST similarity of at least 0.8 as correct gave 84.2% accuracy and a 94.6% true-negative rate but only a 38.4% true-positive rate: the similarity term rejects many functionally equivalent programs. | 2511.19422 — SLMFix: Leveraging Small Language Models for Domain Specific Language Error Fixing with Reinforcement Learning | 13 | For Ansible, we employed the benchmark suite constructed by Councilman et al. (2025), which contains 21 questions and 1,260 outputs, and the comparison between testing result and semantic similarity is shown in Table 7. When considering having a semantic similarity of at least 0.8 as equivalent to the ground-truth, the metric is able to classify most outputs correctly with an accuracy of 84.2% and a true negative rate of 94.6%. However, the true positive rate is only 38.4%, which indicates that the AST-based semantic similarity is too strict and fails to correctly classify Ansible programs that are functionally equivalent to the ground-truth but not semantically equivalent. |

We find the following: - (1) Without deduplication, the fine-tuned StarCoderBase-1B gets a 17.1 pass@1 score on MultiPLE and takes 12 hours to train. - (2) With deduplication, the fine-tuned StarCoderBase-1B gets 17.3 pass@1 on MultiPL-E and takes less than 30 minutes to train. |

| PyLang's GRPO used a fractional reward (tests passed over tests total; 0 for syntax errors and timeouts), 8 samples per step at temperature 0.7, 1,000 Codeforces problems for 4 epochs, β 0.1, learning rate 2 × 10^-6 and full fine-tuning. The paper chose partial credit because fully correct PyLang solutions are rare. | 2605.15607 — Syntax Without Semantics: Teaching Large Language Models to Code in an Unseen Language | 16 | At each training step, the model generates 8 candidate solutions at temperature 0.7 and receives execution-based rewards. The reward function is r = tests\_passed/tests\_total, yielding 1.0 for fully correct solutions, 0.0 for syntax errors, timeouts, or complete failure, and intermediate values for partial correctness. Training uses 1,000 Codeforces problems sampled from the training set, running for 4 epochs with β = 0.1, learning rate 2 × 10-6 , and full fine-tuning. |
